The operator's commands are the other way to run a job: `hirsel store verify DIR --release EXE` walks a copy in `Report` mode, and `hirsel store repair DIR --release EXE` walks and builds a stopped store to the end (STORE-SPEC §15.6, v2.78).
- **Export leases.** An expiry or retention sweep is a data commit like a user's delete, and catch-up replays it (STORE-SPEC §3.5a), so it runs while a lease is held. A build chunk and a walker's demotion each commit a schema change, and they run while a lease is held too: catch-up ships each such commit to the preparation as a step (STORE-SPEC §3.5a "Catch-up", v2.132). A projector step moves its pins and writes rows, so no projector steps under a lease either (v2.80). Scrub never writes and runs regardless.
- **Refusals.** `Poisoned` ends the head as any store fault does. Any other refusal of a job turns that job off until the place reopens, so a failing job never spins the head. A refused projector step turns off that projector alone (v2.80).
- **Upkeep keeps no place awake.** `head_is_idle` (§14.4) does not look at it, so a cold place stops as before. No timer wakes a quiet head for a row's expiry: a due row goes at the head's next idle turn after any input, or at the next open. A row is never deleted before its time, and may outlive it while the place is quiet. A build resumes from its committed cursor at the next open, and a scrub tour or walk starts over.
- **Counts.** The head keeps the rows swept, the tours and walks finished, scrub faults, walk findings and projector steps that had work since it opened, and publishes them as `Status.upkeep: UpkeepCounts {swept, tours, scrub_faults, walks, walk_findings, projected}` beside `Status.store`, whenever they or the attention mark change (v2.78). The head's `StoreAttention` life event carries them too.
- **Law (Store 4.1).** `schedule_laws.hirsel`, on the simulated clock. `expiry_sweep_keeps_time_and_limits`: before a sweep every expired row is visible by scan and point read; each sweep deletes exactly the hand-named rows its row or byte limit allows, in (time, key) order; a row is never deleted one ms before its time and is gone after the first sweep at it; a row with no expiry stays; afterwards a second index agrees and a full walk finds nothing. `head_sweeps_expired_rows`: the head, with nothing calling the sweep, publishes one release per sweep at walls 1199, 1200 and 5000, each serving exactly the rows not yet due. Red on a read-time filter and on a sweep that ignores its limits.
- **Law.** `test_head_builds_demoted_index` (runner-upkeep-law.hirsel): an index demoted before the head serves is `Public` again after the head runs, with no call of the build step in the law, and the head's last release serves it (429 rows under value 3 of 3,000 rows k ↦ k mod 7, by hand). `test_head_sweeps_log_retention` (the same file, Store 1.6): a Log of 10 entries declared `Keep(3)` holds [7, 10) after the head runs, with no call of the sweep in the law, and so does the head's last release (10 − 3 = 7, by hand). `test_head_steps_projector` (v2.80): a projector registered before the head serves has, in the head's last release, one generation holding exactly the source's 300 rows k ↦ k mod 7, with no call of the step in the law; red when the head never steps. The walker's laws are STORE-SPEC L41 (viii) and `test_index_walker_*` (location_laws.hirsel); the interleaved tour is `disk_test_scrub_interleaved`.
- **Law (R4).** `head_interleaves_projector_under_load` queues 33 requests before the head starts. Each request commits its admission and then its write. With no idle turns, the head binds the projector after 32 commits, then publishes a generation covering 32 rows before the 33rd request.

## 14. Cold places under the platform host (new in v2.26)

A place run by the platform host (docs/spec/launch.md "Platform host and launcher") is **cold by default**: it runs only while it has work, and the host starts it again when work comes. This section is the wake contract between the two. The runner's idle stop and child report, the launcher's side of §14.3-§14.5 (`hirsel launcher`, launch.md "The launcher"), and the platform host (`core/programs/platform/platform.hirsel`, `platform_main.hirsel`, `local.hirsel`) are **built**. In-process places run under the same host (§14.9).

The rule in one line: a place that a commit has given a future trigger is woken for it, and the host can always tell, from one durable row per place, whether it knows the place's next due time or must start the place to find out.

### 14.1 The wake row

The host's catalog has one row per authority group (one place) in its `wake` table:

```hirsel
pub type Wake: codec + send = Dirty(u64) | Clean(Option[u64])
pub type WakeRow: codec + send { wake: Wake, gen: u64 }
```

- `Dirty(g)`: the serving child with generation `g` is the **authoritative** child: it may hold, or may have held, the place's store, and the host does not know the place's triggers. The place must run to find out. (v2.27) `g` is at most `gen`; it is less while a cutover's candidate runs (§14.3).
- `Clean(next_due)`: no process holds the store, and `next_due` is the due time of the place's first pending trigger, in `at` units (`store.CallInfo.at`, wall milliseconds), or `None` when it has none that can run.
- `gen` counts the serving launches of the place. It only grows, and each serving launch, a cutover's candidate included, gets the next value.
- **A child's identity** (v2.27) is (group, incarnation, gen, role). `incarnation` is a value of a host-wide counter, written in the group's `groups` row when the group is added and never reused, so a group that is removed and added again under the same name shares no identity with its earlier self, and no late command or report of the earlier one can match it. `role` is the child's mode (launch.md "Child modes"). A `serving` and an `install` child hold the store, so each takes the next `gen` and the one-launch rule of §14.3; other children open no store and are keyed by their deploy attempt in place of `gen`. An install child (genesis, or an operator's standalone install) is the authoritative child while it runs, but it sends no clean report, so its end always leaves the row `Dirty(g)`; the host then starts the serving child at `gen + 1` as a relaunch, or retries the install under §14.5's backoff.
- **A new group.** Adding a group writes `Clean(None)` with `gen` 0 and a new `incarnation`, in the transaction that adds its `groups` row: its store does not exist yet, so it has no trigger. Its genesis is an operator launch and goes through `Dirty` like any other (§14.3).

Every change of a wake row is a transaction of the host's own store. The host waits for that commit to be durable before it acts on it (it launches nothing on a `Dirty` that could still be lost). So every argument below is about the order of the host's commits and of the facts the launcher reports, never about the order of tasks in one run (CONC-10, SEM-8 and CAP-8 promise none that this needs).

### 14.2 Capacity

The host's `config` row holds `max_places`, and the `wake` table keeps a count `dirty` of its `Dirty` rows, updated in the same transaction as every row change.

- **Check before `Dirty`.** A transaction that turns a `Clean` row into `Dirty` first reads `dirty`. If `dirty` ≥ `max_places`, it commits nothing and the start is refused `Full(Count(max_places))`. Otherwise it writes `Dirty(gen + 1)`, raises `gen` and `dirty`, and commits. A row that is already `Dirty` is re-marked `Dirty(gen + 1)` without a check, because `dirty` does not change, but only when the host launches a new serving child for it after the authoritative one has ended: a probe, or a relaunch after an arrival during a drain (§14.3). A cutover's candidate raises `gen` and leaves the row `Dirty(g)` until the cutover commits (§14.3). A wake of a group with an outstanding launch changes nothing (§14.3, the table).
- **The invariant.** `dirty` ≤ `max_places` holds in every committed state, because the host's store runs one writer and every transaction that raises `dirty` checks it.
- **Lowering the bound.** A `config` change that sets `max_places` below the current `dirty` is refused `Busy(dirty)`. The operator stops places first. So the invariant holds across changes, and no rule reads a historical bound.
- **No eviction.** A full host refuses or queues a start. It never stops a live place to make room.
- Each tenant's live-group quota is checked in the same transaction, the same way.
- **The host's own group** (v2.27) is not counted in `dirty` or in any tenant's quota, and it has no idle stop: it owns the wake timer and the queue, so it runs for as long as the platform does.

### 14.3 Waking

A place is woken by any of these:

- **Arrival.** The launcher reports `Arrived(group)` when a cold group's listener becomes readable. The connection waits in the backlog, and the new process accepts it from the inherited listener (launch.md "Routing").
- **Due time.** The host keeps an index of `Clean(Some(t))` rows by `t`. It waits for the earliest `t` with an absolute wall-time wait on its `Clock`, and wakes that place when the wall clock reads `t` or later. A trigger is due when the next commit's `at` is at least its due time (ENDSTATE T1), and that `at` is at least the wall clock, so the trigger is due when the woken head reads it. A wake that comes early because the clock stepped back is harmless: the head arms its timer for the trigger as at any open, and the idle rule (§14.4) keeps the place up until it runs.
- **Operator or deploy.** A deploy, an install, a genesis or an operator request to a cold place first wakes it. Every launch that opens the place's store goes through `Dirty`, so `next_due` is never stale after one of them.

**What a wake does, by the group's state.** The host knows a group's state from its own records and the launcher's events (§14.5), never from the child's phase. A serving launch is **outstanding** from the host's request until the launcher reports its end; between them the group is live, whether its child is not yet started, `Starting`, `Ready` or `Draining`.

| Group state (host) | Row | Arrival | Due time | Deploy or operator request | The authoritative child ends with a clean report for its `g` | It ends otherwise |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cold | `Clean(t)` | the check of §14.2, then `Dirty(gen + 1)` and a launch; on `Full` queued (below) | the same | the check, then wake first and act; on `Full` refused `Full`, which the caller may retry | (no child) | (no child) |
| Live, one outstanding launch `g` | `Dirty(g)` | coalesced: the launcher watches a listener only while its group has no outstanding serving launch | coalesced: the index holds `Clean` rows only, and the live head arms its own | acts on the live child; a deploy starts a cutover | `Clean(next_due)`, unless the end report says the listener is readable: then `Dirty(gen + 1)` and a relaunch | stays `Dirty(g)`; a probe at `gen + 1` (§14.5, backoff) |
| Cutover, OLD `g` and candidate `c` | `Dirty(g)` | not reported | none | refused `Busy` until the cutover ends | held until the cutover decides (below) | held until the cutover decides |
| Failing (§14.5), no child | `Dirty(g)` | coalesced into the scheduled retry | the same | an operator retry starts it now; a deploy; a retirement (§14.5) | (no child) | (no child) |
| Retiring (§14.5) | `Dirty(g)` | dropped | dropped | refused `Busy` | applied, then retirement goes on | applied, then retirement goes on |

**One serving launch at a time (v2.27).** A group has at most one outstanding serving launch, and two during a cutover: OLD and one candidate. Every wake cause of a group with an outstanding launch is coalesced into it and changes nothing. A launch request is keyed by the child's identity (§14.1). A repeated request with the same key is answered with that child's current state and starts nothing, so a host that is unsure whether its request arrived may send it again. The launcher enforces the same bound: it refuses a serving launch for a (group, incarnation) that has a serving child or an accepted start, unless the request is a candidate that names that child's `g` and no other candidate exists. So two serving launches of one group are never requested by mistake, and STORE's `LOCK` is the last check, not the only one. On objects@1 there is no such last check: a later claim deposes the earlier holder, which then stops on `Fenced` (§2.1). So this rule is the guard there, and a violation costs a deposed head and its in-flight calls, never two heads that both commit.

**Cutover commit and abort (v2.27).** A deploy launches NEW as a candidate at `c = gen + 1`, raising `gen`, while the row stays `Dirty(g)` naming OLD. NEW is prestarted with the deploy's activation `v` as its selection. OLD's head does the cutover: under its write pause it imports the prepared target and installs `v` under `LOCK` (on objects@1, by the activation CHECKPOINT's head write, STORE §12.7), and it answers the host `Installed(v)`, `Drift` or `Refused` (launch.md "Hot deploy"). Until the cutover decides, OLD is the authoritative child, and the host applies no report of NEW's.
- **Preparing.** Before any of this the deploy is `Preparing`. The host commits a row naming the attempt, NEW's assembly record, NEW's image digest, and OLD's `g`, then releases the attempt's reservation. In order, it runs OLD's describe child, then NEW's preview child on the exact bytes of OLD's exported list. It checks that the preview names NEW's image digest, the verified target and `n_place`, an entry revision (v2.42), a 32-byte release, and one answer per old entry. Only then does it send OLD `HostExport`. On `PlaceExported(head, name)` with a valid name it runs NEW's prepare child on that export. After that child exits 0 it sends OLD `HostImport` with NEW's exact `Describe` bytes, the release and the image digest, and (v2.82) the request's approved fates and whether it is an editor's. OLD validates PREPARED as STORE §3.5a M1–M3 says and, from the same bytes, reports the migration fact (each 0x0082 payload and the 0x0083 (id, kind) list) on `PlaceImported`. OLD also checks the settlement, because the prepare child runs submitted code: with no fates, an editor's PREPARED that carries a 0x0085 frame or ships a range of `store.triggers.pending` is refused; any rewrite of another table is refused; with fates, OLD classifies its own pending rows at the exported base under NEW's identity digest and entries, settles each by the fates (`fate.settle`), and refuses unless PREPARED applies exactly those rows, in 0x0085 or in a shipped range of `store.triggers.pending`. A refusal is `PlaceRefused`. On `PlaceImported(digest, migration)` one transaction re-checks that the row is still `Preparing` for this attempt under `Dirty(g)`. It allocates `c`, records `v = head + 1`, the prepared digest and the release, and makes the row `Pending`. The row also records the identity digests (§6 "One identity", v2.42) of OLD (from its describe child) and NEW (from the preview) on every backend (v2.37), and on objects@1 OLD's pair, which `PlaceImported` carries there: `store.claim_of` on OLD's Db, fixed for OLD's open (STORE §12.3; v2.36). Only then do the candidate's `Start` and the cutover go out. A failure at any step (a refused or failed helper, a helper that ends without its answer, a check, `PlaceRefused`, OLD's end) stops the helper. Once the export was asked for, it also sends OLD `HostAbort`. The row stays `Preparing` until OLD answers `PlaceAborted` or ends and the helper has ended, and only then records `Failed`. A new session finds a `Preparing` row with no cutover sent. If OLD runs, the session sends it `HostAbort` and records `Failed(interrupted before the cutover)` on its answer; if OLD is gone, it records that failure at once. A `Preparing` deploy keeps the group `Busy` for another deploy. Recovery of a drift retry
sends `HostAbort(retry, Some(original))`. OLD releases the export and write pause matching
either attempt and answers `PlaceAborted` after applying the abort.
- **Commit.** OLD's head answers `Installed(v)`. The host writes `Dirty(c)` and the deploy row's outcome in one transaction. OLD then retires and closes the store, and NEW opens it at `v`. From then on OLD's reports name `g` and change nothing, and an end of OLD that was held is dropped.
- **An unknown install (v2.34, objects@1).** If OLD's install returns `Store(Fenced(e))` or `Store(Undecided)`, the activation may or may not have applied (STORE §12.7). OLD's head answers nothing and ends abnormally (§2.1), the launcher reports `Uncertain`, which counts as OLD's end, and the deploy takes the **Undecided** path below. The attempt is decided at `v` (the exported main head + 1 the host recorded, never OLD's activation + 1) by comparing `releases[v].prepared` with this attempt's prepared digest (never `releases[v].digest`, which is the image digest; STORE v2.49 §12.7), from one capture: a probe's open, or after a refused open the probe's read below. OLD never answers `Refused` for it.
- **Abort.** OLD's head answers `Refused` (only for a failure before its activation head write, STORE §12.7), or `Drift` with no retry left. The first `Drift` of a deploy is its one retry: OLD keeps its write pause, the deploy row returns to `Preparing` at a fresh attempt with the original attempt recorded, and OLD exports again as a retry under that pause; the candidate is discarded as below, and once OLD keeps the retry's import and the discarded candidate has ended, a new one starts at the next counter value (launch.md "Hot deploy"). The store's current activation is still OLD's, and `v` was never installed. The host sends `AbortCutover`, records the failure in the deploy row, and discards the candidate; the row stays `Dirty(g)`. A discarded candidate cannot have changed the store: its selection names `v`, so if it ever takes `LOCK` it is refused `NotCurrent` before its handles run (§12.1, `Activating`). So OLD's later clean report is still exact, and the candidate's end clears nothing and restarts nothing; it is not OLD's end.
- **Undecided.** OLD's head gave no answer (OLD ended, or the session was lost in the pause). If the candidate reports that it opened the store at `v` with the release the preview reported **and** `releases[v].prepared` equal to the attempt's prepared digest (v2.35), that proves the install, and the cutover commits. An open at `v` whose `prepared` differs proves that this attempt did not install: the deploy is recorded failed (`not installed after recovery`), and what serves follows "What serves" below, as for a probe that opens. Otherwise the host does not guess: both ends are discarded, the row stays `Dirty(g)`, and the deploy is `Undecided` with both roots kept. The group is probed at a new generation with NEW's assembly at `v` first (launch.md "When the host starts"). Every probe of an Undecided deploy names the attempt's `v` as its **witness version**, apart from its selection.
  - **A probe that opens** decides: its claim changed the pair, so `releases[v]` present with the attempt's digest is Installed, and present with another `prepared` or absent is failed. An open at `v` that proves the install commits NEW as above: this attempt wrote that activation with NEW's `Describe`, so the commit writes NEW's record into the activation index at `v`. Any other open records the outcome and keeps serving: its open matched the store's identity, so "What serves" selects the opened record, the start's own, at the opened activation (v2.42).
  - **The probe's read (v2.36; log@1 v2.37).** A probe refused `NotCurrent` takes, before it exits and without opening application handles, one capture of the store (STORE §12.7), and its `PlaceCurrent` carries the store ID, the activation a, the identity digest (v2.42: on objects@1 HEAD's 32-byte field, on log@1 the identity digest of `current`'s `Describe`), on objects@1 the pair, and the row's `prepared` or "absent".
    - **On objects@1** the capture is a follower: `follower(mut source, take slot, take a, clock, expect)` with `expect` set to the store ID the host recorded (`None` on a start with no recorded ID), on the slot and Authority that "The capture's service" below gives it, then `fresh` with the deadline `op_ms` from now (`BucketBacking.op_ms`), then `follower_head`, `releases[witness]` and (v2.39) the activation table's `current`, all from that one fresh head. The read's `Describe` is what `current` decodes to, for the cause's text, or "does not decode" when its bytes do not decode as a `Describe` or its identity digest is not the head's (STORE §12.7).
    - **The capture's service (v2.54, STORE v2.69 B).** The head that took the refused open provisions its capture's follower itself, in its own task. Nothing else in the Runner starts a `FollowerService`. In order:
      1. **What it holds.** The head keeps the Authority that its refused open hands back in its `Refusal` (STORE v2.70 §1.1, D1), and moves it into `follower`, so it and its client's lanes are dropped with the Follower. Every failed open hands its Authority back with its tasks joined and its other reservations refunded, and an open refused by STORE §12.1's checks also claims nothing and writes nothing. So of the head's store term only the client lanes L are still held, by that Authority's client. The capture uses that client as its transport, which is the F = T − L that STORE §1.1 assumes. The head also holds the `FollowerPlan` that `store.planned_capture` returned from the same plan (§12.1 "Store sizes", v2.56).
      2. **Start.** `follower_service(tasks, service_bytes([plan]))`, then `follower_owner`, then `follower_slot(owner, plan)`, then `store.followers()` as the head's one `Followers` source (the head is its process's only follower opener), then `follower` and the reads above. No step bound is current: the head is `operate`'s child, and `operate` is entered with none (§12.1 "Tasks"). So the worker runs outside every bound, and the head takes only the steps of its own requests and waits.
      3. **Close.** After the read the head drops the Follower, the owner and the source and calls `close_follower_service`, which joins the worker and the service task. Only then does it send `PlaceCurrent` and end. So every byte of the service is back in the head before the report.
      4. **Bytes.** The head's peak during the capture is L + (S + F) + R = T + X, its allowance's store term. That is `store.planned_bytes`, and `place_store` already admitted it at start, so a limit that passes `place_store` also fits the capture. An undersized limit is refused at `place_store`, naming T + X, before any child task, client or claim (§12.1; law L60).
      5. **One per start.** A start captures at most once, so it has at most one service. For a place child, whose head is its process's only store owner, that is one service per process owner.

      The witness-less read of "Leaving no record" is this same capture with its `releases` row left out, so it is provisioned the same way.
    - **On log@1** the capture is the probe's own open: it keeps `LOCK` after the refused activation and `Describe` comparison until it has checked the recorded store ID (§14.5) and read `activation`, `current` and `releases[witness]` from its recovered state, and only then releases it (STORE §12.7 "On log@1").
    - A failed read (on objects@1 `Remote`, `Full`, `Foreign`, `Reclaimed`, `Behind`, and since v2.54 a refusal of the capture's service, slot or follower: `Full(Allocation(c))`, `Retired` (STORE v2.71), `Missing("follower_kind")`; on log@1 a failed bind or read) reports `PlaceCurrent` with no read, which decides nothing and is retried under the ordinary backoff. A start refused `Held`, or answered `PlaceStoreMismatch`, reads nothing.
  - **The outcome**, from the read by STORE §12.7, in one transaction: the attempt's digest is Installed; another `prepared` is failed (`not installed after recovery`), and so is an absent row on log@1, whose read held `LOCK` after recovery's directory barrier, or on objects@1 an absent row under another pair than OLD's recorded pair. On objects@1 an absent row under OLD's pair stays Undecided: OLD's delayed head write can still land, so the next probe selects OLD's pair at OLD's activation, and its open (a claim, which ends that write for good) or its read decides. Row `v` decides even when a is above `v`. **Installed records NEW at `v` (v2.38, astra 1a):** the same transaction writes NEW's record into the activation index at `v`, because this attempt wrote `v` with NEW's identity, exactly as an opening probe's commit does. So a probe refused before OLD's delayed write landed, whose read then sees D at `v`, leaves the same catalog as a probe that opened at `v`. When a is `v`, "What serves" then makes NEW current at `v`; when a is above `v`, NEW at `v` is a retained activation and row a's identity alone decides what is current.
  - **What serves**, recorded in the same transaction as a decided outcome and never derived from it: activation a, with a record whose recorded identity digest equals the read's (v2.42). The candidates are this deploy's OLD and NEW that the group still roots. Two records can share an identity (the same code assembled by another backend, or a recipe that changed only grants or bindings), so the rule is one function of durable facts and asks no one:
    - **One record matches**: it serves a.
    - **Both match** (two records, one identity): the deploy's outcome, decided in this same transaction, picks. Installed serves NEW; failed serves OLD. An Undecided outcome selects nothing and the next probe decides. When OLD and NEW are one record, it matches once.
    - **No record** (another install's identity: other code, another `n_place`, or another exported list): nothing is selected. The same transaction writes the group's **no-record mark** (v2.39; two states since v2.40), `recovery` in its `groups` row, as `Unmatched(attempt, a, identity, n_place)`: this deploy's attempt, the read's activation a, the read's identity digest and the allowance its `current` names (or "does not decode"). The group is failing with the cause `No recovery candidate matches the store at activation a` (v2.40), then `: its release identity is <hex> at n_place <bytes>` or `does not decode`, then `; candidates: <record hex> with identity <hex> at n_place <bytes>, ...` for this deploy's OLD and NEW that the group still roots after this transaction. It recovers through its own retries ("Leaving no record" below). `Status` formats this cause from the mark itself (§14.5 "Failing and backoff"). This holds for an Installed outcome as for a failed one: an Installed attempt whose store has since moved to an unmatched activation is marked too.

    With nothing selected the recorded activation is unchanged. So an Installed attempt whose store has since moved to `v + 1` serves `v + 1`, never `v`, and a failed one never selects OLD's activation once another install moved the head. The next start's open (§12.1, STORE §12.1) stays the guard either way: a retry of a failing group at its recorded activation is refused `NotCurrent` before handles, and on objects@1 before any claim, so its application and activation write nothing (on log@1 the refused open's own recovery may do its I/O).
  - **Roots** (the one statement of this rule; every other place refers here). The selected assembly is current. The other is retained when the deploy installed or when NEW was selected. A failed deploy drops its claim on NEW only when OLD was selected to serve. With nothing selected, OLD stays current and NEW retained, in the group's own rows, since a decided deploy row roots nothing (launch.md "Collect"). An adoption ("Leaving no record") is a selection under this rule: the record it adopts is current, and the deploy's outcome decides whether the other stays.
  - **Leaving no record (v2.39, astra m2-v251-astra-1961 item 3).** A marked group keeps retrying under the ordinary backoff (§14.5), and a host restart probes it like any `Dirty` row. Each serving start of a marked group still selects the group's recorded activation and record, so it is refused there as above. Refused, it takes **the probe's read without a witness**: the same one capture as "The probe's read", reading the store ID, the activation a′, the identity digest and `current`, but no `releases` row. On objects@1 that is a follower, which claims nothing and writes nothing, provisioned by "The capture's service" (v2.54); on log@1 the probe keeps `LOCK` after the refused comparison, checks the recorded store ID and reads its recovered state before it releases `LOCK`. Its `PlaceCurrent` carries the read. The host compares the read's identity digest with the identity digests of the mark's attempt's OLD and NEW, which that deploy row already records, considering only a record that is still a root of the group, and picks among two matches as "What serves" does, by that deploy's decided outcome (v2.42):
    - **One is picked** and it is the group's recorded activation and current record: nothing changes, and the group stays on the ordinary backoff (the refusal raced a write that has since been undone or was transient).
    - **One is picked**, and (a′, record) differs from the group's recorded activation and current record: one transaction **adopts** it. It re-checks that the group has the same incarnation and the same mark, no retiring mark and no `Preparing`, `Pending` or `Undecided` deploy, and that the report comes from the group's outstanding serving generation. It makes that record current at a′, writes it into the activation index at a′, applies "Roots" with it selected (a failed deploy whose OLD is adopted drops NEW, and otherwise both stay), and turns the mark into `Adopted(attempt, a′, record)` (v2.40). The deploy's terminal outcome is unchanged, and so is an Installed outcome's index entry at `v`. The start's end then relaunches the group at `gen + 1` at a′ at once, outside backoff, as a genesis adoption does (§14.5). The mark stays, in either state, until a start's `PlaceOpened` is applied, which clears it in the same transaction, so a relaunch refused again (the store moved once more) reads and decides again by these same three cases, from either state. An `Adopted` mark carries no cause: the store matched, so a relaunch that then fails shows its own refusal or exit status (§14.5 "Failing and backoff").
    - **None matches**: the selection is left alone, and the mark becomes `Unmatched` with the new a′, identity digest and allowance (from either state), so the cause stays current. The group stays failing on backoff.
    - **No read** (a failed read, `Held`, `PlaceStoreMismatch`, or an end before any `PlaceCurrent`) decides nothing and keeps the mark and its cause.

    So the operator's way out is a hand install of one candidate's release (launch.md "Place activation"): `./place --hirsel-n-place=<bytes> --hirsel-install`, run from an executable of that candidate's code (any backend: the identity is the code's, v2.42) at the allowance the cause prints, with the private option before `--hirsel-install`<!-- doc-ref: external --> (§6). The install registers and describes at that allowance (§6 "One n_place"), its identity digest is the candidate's, and the next retry's read adopts the record "What serves" picks. The adoption grants nothing: the read's identity must equal a digest the host itself recorded for an approved record of this group, and the record that serves is the host's, launched with its own image and grants. No operator request chooses a record, so a deploy's `Select` does not return. A retirement removes the mark with the group.
- **Across sessions.** The deploy row is durable before the candidate's `Start` and the cutover command, and it holds that command's arguments: the attempt, the image digest, OLD's `g` and the candidate's `c`. Each new session's inventory resumes every row still pending. With the candidate in the inventory, the host sends the identical cutover command again: the launcher forwards it if it never did, stays silent while OLD holds it, and otherwise returns OLD's kept answer, or `Uncertain` if OLD ended first, which counts as OLD's end. With OLD running and no candidate, the candidate's `Start` goes out first, then the command. While the launcher holds OLD's answer, the ends of OLD and the candidate are recorded and decide nothing until that answer is applied. With neither child and no held answer, nothing proves the install: the outcome is `Undecided` and the group is probed. OLD therefore installs at most once per attempt whatever the host loses. The host sends `CutoverAck` only after its commit, abort or undecided outcome is durable, and acknowledges any held cutover that names no pending row. A launcher lost after OLD installed `v` leaves neither child nor held answer: the probe of NEW at `v` opens and commits NEW, with no cutover sent again; one lost before the install gets `PlaceCurrent` from that probe and the open of OLD's pair records the failure. The platform harness checks each loss point in `cutover_survives_host_loss`. Each objects@1 read in it goes through "The capture's service", and the harness checks that the service is closed before `PlaceCurrent` reaches the host (v2.54). It also asserts each recovery Start's generation, activation and assembly, and the catalog's root after the probe's answer. After each outcome a collection through the host keeps exactly the roots of "Roots" above, and an Undecided or Preparing deploy keeps both (launch.md "Collect"). The harness also checks the moved-activation histories of STORE L54 (v2.36), its history (ii′) and its log@1 histories (v2.37), and its refusal, write and follower order (v2.38): after a host restart, the read decides each, and a collection keeps the hand-derived roots. Since v2.42 it also checks a deploy whose OLD and NEW share one identity (NEW changes only a binding): after each loss point the read matches both, and the record that serves is NEW for an Installed outcome and OLD for a failed one. It also checks L54's no-record recovery (v2.39): the hand install of a retained record at a later activation, a host restart, the retry's read adopting that record, serving there, and a collection. Since v2.40 that history runs with the records' hosted allowance different from the place's `PlaceConfig.n_place`, so the hand install adopts only when it registered and described at the launch's allowance; it checks `Status.failing[].last` during backoff after a retry that ends before any `PlaceCurrent` and after a host restart that recovers such an end from the inventory (the mark's cause, then the exit status), and after an adoption whose relaunch dies before `PlaceOpened` (the exit status alone); and an Undecided probe at `Current(10)` refused at 3 with a failed read reports that it requested 10. Since v2.41 the allowances are 524,288 configured and 1,048,576 hosted; since v2.42 the hand install's argv is `./place --hirsel-n-place=1048576 --hirsel-install`, run through the native entry point (and in the other order, an install at `PlaceConfig.n_place` that matches no record), and two histories with the same `Unmatched` mark and the same generation launched at 3 differ only in whether the child reported `PlaceCurrent(11)` with a failed read before exiting 1 while the host was down: after the restart, `last` ends with that start's refusal in the first and with `exit status 1` in the second.

**Event order (v2.27).** The launcher's events reach the host as one ordered stream per control session (§14.5), and the host applies the events of one group in stream order; it may interleave groups. When a serving child ends, the launcher first samples its listener for readability, then sends the child's `End` with that answer in it, and only then starts its level-triggered watch of the listener. So an `Arrived` for the group can only follow that `End` in the stream, and the host never takes an `Arrived` while it still counts the ended child as outstanding. Each inventory (§14.5) repeats every readable cold listener, so a readable listener is reported again after a reconnect. No order across groups is needed.

**An arrival during a drain.** A drain closes admission (§12.1; an idle stop has already closed it at its confirmation, §14.4), so a connection that arrives after admission closes waits in the listener's backlog, which the launcher still holds. It is in the `End` report or a later `Arrived`, and the next process answers it. Neither waits behind a `Clean` row. A connection that arrives before admission closes may be answered by the draining child.

**Queued wakes (v2.27).** A wake runs the check of §14.2. On success the host launches the serving child with the selected activation, and the launcher tags the child with its identity. On `Full`, a due time or an arrival is queued in the host's memory, one entry per group with its earliest cause, in (time of that cause, group name) order. An operator or deploy request is never queued: it is refused `Full`, and the caller retries it, so the queue holds only causes the host can rebuild.
- The host retries the queue on every change that can make room: a row leaving `Dirty`, a raised `max_places`, a raised tenant quota, and a retirement. It tries each queued group in order, independently: one that still does not fit (its tenant is still full) stays queued and does not block the ones after it.
- The due-time wait always follows the minimum of the index of `Clean(Some(t))` rows that are not queued. It is recomputed whenever that minimum changes: an insert below it, an insert into an empty index, or a removal. The host's driver owns this wait and the backoff wait and re-arms both after every input it applies (launch.md "Running the host"), so a minimum that changes after start is never missed. A row that is overdue and queued has left the wait, so it cannot spin the timer or hold back a later row of another group.
- After a host restart the queue is rebuilt from what is durable or observable: each row of the due index whose `t` has passed, and each readable cold listener of the inventory. Nothing else was in it.
- The trigger then runs late, which ENDSTATE T2 already allows: it runs once, and sees how late it is from its own `at`. The host reports each queued wake and its age in its status, so a lateness is never hidden.

### 14.4 The idle stop and the clean report

**Idle (extends §12.1).** `PlaceConfig` gains `idle: Option[u64]`, an idle time in milliseconds; `Some(0)` is refused `Missing("idle")`. With `Some(i)`, `operate` stops the place itself when it is idle for `i`. A part is **idle** when:
- S holds no session;
- H holds no worker and no FIFO entry, its trigger drive has no active run, and it holds no export lease (§5 "Prepared deployment"; a hot deploy's export keeps its place up until the cutover or the attempt ends);
- H has no pending trigger due before `at` + `i`, where `at` is `store.next_call_info(db).at`, among the rows `triggers.next_pending` returns: serving StoreId, branch 0, not active, and not parked unless its cancel is requested (v2.27; a parked row is skipped as at dispatch, and a cancelling one is finished at its due time).

**The idle check (v2.27).** H and S send `operate` a message each time their part becomes idle or busy. `operate` arms a deadline `i` after both have reported idle. A busy report cancels the deadline. These messages are only hints: a part can start work after it sent its last one. When the deadline expires, `operate` asks the parts themselves, S first and then H:
1. S answers from its own state. If it holds no session, it interrupts A's pending accept, waits until A has parked before its next accept, and confirms. From then on a connection waits in the listener's backlog, which the next process accepts (launch.md "Routing"). A connection that A took before it parked is a session, and S answers busy.
2. After S confirms, `operate` asks H. H answers after it has handled every input it took before the ask, from its own state, by the rule above. If it is idle it confirms and then admits nothing until `operate` answers: it takes no request, starts no trigger attempt and grants no export lease. A trigger start or a lease that H made before the ask makes it answer busy.
3. When both confirm, `operate` takes the stop as `StopRequested`, by the path of §12.1 "Draining". Admission is already closed: A stays parked and is dropped when admission closes, so the place accepts no connection after S confirmed, and a connection in the backlog is answered by the next process (§14.3). H leaves its parked state only to drain.
4. When either answers busy, `operate` releases the part that confirmed, resets the interval and keeps serving.

So an idle stop never cuts a run or a lease that started before it was decided, and nothing starts after it. An active granted run therefore keeps its place up. Without this rule, a drain would cut the run and the next start would run attempt n + 1, so a cold place would churn its attempts.

**Where `idle` comes from.** The platform host sets an idle time for every serving launch of a platform place. The launcher starts the place as a **wake launch**: it puts `--hirsel-wake --hirsel-idle=<ms>`<!-- doc-ref: external -->, with a nonzero idle time, in the runtime prefix, before `--hirsel-activation`<!-- doc-ref: external --> and the program's arguments, and `runner.main` passes that time to `operate` in place of `PlaceConfig.idle`. An idle time of 0 is refused as `Some(0)` is. Every other launch of `runner.main`, and the fixture, use `PlaceConfig.idle` as given, which is `None` unless the program sets it.

**Unload (v2.31).** `platform.unload(name)` asks a group's live serving child to stop. It is refused `Unknown` for no group, `Retiring` for a group being retired, `Busy` while a cutover holds a candidate, and `Missing("live")` when no child runs. Otherwise the host sends `Stop` to the serving child of the current incarnation and generation, which drains as an idle stop does (§12.1 "Draining"). The end is applied by the clean-report rule below: a clean `Stopped(next_due)` writes `Clean(next_due)`, any other end leaves `Dirty` and probes. An arrival during the drain relaunches at gen + 1 (§14.3, L46). So an unload changes when the place runs, never whether a due trigger runs.

**The clean report (extends §12.1).** A head that ends on a failed `durable` or on a store health other than `Ok` sends none (§2.1). `Ended` becomes `Stopped(Option[u64]) | Unstarted | Forced(u64)`. On the drain's finish (§12.1 step 6), after the last commit is durable and before `store.close`, H reads `triggers.next_pending` on its last snapshot with no active run. It sends that row's due time, or `None`, to `operate` in its last message, and `operate` returns `Ok(Stopped(next_due))`. A parked trigger is skipped unless its cancel is requested, as at dispatch: only a release or a cancel, each a transaction through an entry and so a wake, can make it runnable. A run that a drain cut is pending again, so its due time is in the past and the place is woken at once. A head that runs no trigger drive (§12.1: no row and no entry) returns `Stopped(None)`.

`runner.main` started as a wake launch writes `next_due` on inherited descriptor 5, the launcher's private report descriptor (the listener and the control socket stay on descriptors 3 and 4), when it gets `Stopped(next_due)`, as one value-codec `Option[u64]`, and exits 0. It writes nothing for `Unstarted`, `Forced` or a `StartError`. The launcher forwards the report with the child's `gen` and its exit status.

**Only a clean `Stopped` writes `Clean`.** The host writes `Clean(next_due)` in one transaction that requires the report to come from a `serving` child of the group's current incarnation, the row to be `Dirty(g)` with the report's `gen` equal to `g`, no candidate to be outstanding, the exit status to be 0 and the report to be present, and it lowers `dirty`. Any other end leaves the row `Dirty(gen)`: a crash, a kill, `Unstarted`, `Forced`, a `StartError`, an exit 0 with no report, a report from an older generation, or a lost report. The generation fence matters at a cutover: once NEW has committed, the row names NEW, so OLD's later report names the old `g` and changes nothing; before that, OLD's report is held (§14.3).

### 14.5 Crashes, restarts and probes

The host durably records the store ID from the first accepted `PlaceOpened` for each group
incarnation. A hosted runner waits for `HostStoreAccepted` before serving. The launcher
repeats an unaccepted `PlaceOpened` after the next session's inventory. Subsequent serving starts carry that expected ID in their private launch
metadata. After opening the store under LOCK, the runner compares the ID before it
prepares the activation, opens application handles, or serves. On objects@1 it passes the expected ID as `o.expect`, so the store refuses another store with `Foreign`, and an empty namespace with `NotFound`, before any write (STORE §12.1); the runner reports both as below. On a mismatch it sends
`PlaceStoreMismatch(expected_hi, expected_lo, actual_hi, actual_lo)` and exits. The
launcher logs the group and both IDs. The host rejects a `PlaceOpened` with a different
store ID as recovery evidence.

**Adopting a restored child.** A writable restore creates a child StoreId even for a backup at Latest (STORE §15.3). The operator adds a fresh group incarnation targeting the verified child Directory or standalone Bucket prefix. Record the expected child ID from the verified restore, its destination, and the selected activation and matching release assembly in the launch intent. Keep the previous incarnation pinned to its own store. Reuse of its name or listener waits for its retirement to reach Retired.

1. Wake the new row from Clean, committing the selection and Dirty generation before Start. Pass the expected child ID on the first open as well as subsequent opens. For an activated child select its restored activation, including when an older release is needed. Compare its release binding/Describe to the selected assembly. Only a genuinely unactivated child uses genesis. The restore receipt grants no claim authority.
2. For a streamed Bucket, start from the child's verified standalone head. Check identity, namespace, activation, release and the authorized current epoch/holder pair before writing. Use the ordinary slot for this child StoreId and next epoch, conditional ATTACH with an empty sealed list, and slot CLAIM acknowledgement (STORE §12.11). Managed starts use their authorized ClaimAuth. An initial operator adoption uses the existing authorization path. The parent's slot, claim and stream tail cannot be reused.
3. Apply PlaceOpened only to the intended live incarnation and generation. Check the observed ID against the restore intent. In one host transaction record the fresh StoreId and matching activation/release and applicable claim, then send HostStoreAccepted. On mismatch refuse before opening service. A host lost before the transaction rechecks the repeated report; after commit it can acknowledge that same child from inventory. Later starts require the recorded ID. Service, routing, follower and client rebinding wait for acceptance. Old Positions belong to the parent.

**The control session (v2.27).** The host talks to the launcher over one control session at a time, on the launcher's control socket (launch.md "One control socket").
- A session starts with the host's `Hello`. The launcher first ends any earlier session: it stops reading the old connection, and each request of it that it had not acted on is discarded, never acted on later. Only then does it answer.
- Its answer is the **inventory**, taken in the one loop in which the launcher changes its state: every child it has, by identity (§14.1) and with its state (accepted and not yet started, started, or ended with an end the host has not acknowledged, with the end's report); for each serving child, the activation and assembly record its `Start` selected; for an ended serving child, its **last answer** (v2.41, astra m2-v253-astra-1967 item 3): the last `PlaceCurrent` (with the read it carries, if any), refusal or `PlaceStoreMismatch` its endpoint sent before it ended, kept with the end until the end is acknowledged; every cold listener that is readable; and no collection fence (a new session drops it, launch.md "Collect").
- Every event the launcher sends afterwards follows the inventory in the same stream. Each state change happens in that one loop, so each is either in the inventory or after it, never both and never neither. The launcher keeps an ended child in the inventory until the host acknowledges its end, so an end is not lost with a session. For an ended child listed with a last answer, the host applies that answer by the rules for it ("Genesis", §14.3 "The probe's read", "Leaving no record") before it applies the end and acknowledges it. So a host lost after the start reported but before its end was acknowledged learns the same answer, and the cause formatted from the child's own selection, as a host that stayed up. Applying an answer a second time changes nothing: a read already applied finds the deploy decided or the mark already at that read (after an adoption the adopted pair is the group's recorded activation and current record, which is "One matches" with nothing to change), and the start's own outcome is the same value.
- The inventory also lists each cutover command the launcher forwarded to an OLD child that the host has not acknowledged, keyed by (OLD child, attempt). The launcher keeps OLD's first answer, or `Uncertain` if OLD ended without one, until `CutoverAck`. A re-sent identical cutover command for that key is answered with the kept reply as `CutoverResult`, never forwarded again, and a different command for the same key is refused. So a host that lost a cutover's reply with its session learns the decided answer instead of repeating the install.
- A new session ends every reservation and every running admission of the old one. The host has already made each attempt whose pending row is durable a root through that row, and it abandons each attempt whose row is not ("When the host starts"); no collection runs before that reconciliation.
- A child in the inventory that matches no row (an old incarnation, a removed group, or a generation that is neither the authoritative child nor a candidate of a pending deploy) is stopped by its identity, and nothing it reports is applied.
- A launch request that the host sent in an earlier session and that is not in the inventory will never run. So the host may start a new generation without fearing a late start of an older one: a command is fenced at the launcher before its child can open the store.

**A host restart (the launcher lives).** Only the host's process ended, and the launcher starts it again as its first group: at once the first time, then after a doubling delay from 1 s up to 5 min, reset by a 5 min run, and never after status 64 or 74 (launch.md "Starting the host"). Live groups keep serving, and cold listeners keep their backlogs. The host reconciles in this order:
1. It opens a session and takes the inventory.
2. It **adopts** each serving child whose (incarnation, `gen`) is the row's authoritative child: the group is live, nothing is interrupted, and the child's later end and report are applied as usual. It acknowledges each ended child and applies its end as §14.3 and §14.4 say.
3. It reconciles each pending deploy (launch.md "When the host starts"). A candidate of that deploy found in the inventory is adopted and its cutover goes on (§14.3).
4. Each `Dirty` row that has no adopted child and no accepted start is a **probe**. Each readable listener and each passed due time of a `Clean` row is a wake (§14.3, "Queued wakes").

**A launcher loss.** The launcher's process ended, so every listener it held is closed and its children lost their parent. The host is one of them: it is the launcher's first group, so it is ended with the rest and started again by the new launcher, and it never reconnects across a launcher loss.
- The new launcher first **fences** the old one: it takes the launcher lock, an OS lock on a file in its state directory that a launcher holds for its whole life, and the lock is free only once no earlier launcher process is alive.
- Under cgroup delegation, the successor finds the cgroups named by child identity in its state directory's subtree, kills their tasks and waits for them to empty. It removes staging before answering `Hello` with an empty inventory.
- Without delegation, a child can survive launcher loss. A survivor can hold the store's `LOCK`, so its group's probe is refused `Held` and the group is failing until it dies. On objects@1 a probe whose opening checks pass claims and deposes the survivor, whose next head write or poll ends it on `Fenced` (§2.1). A probe refused by those checks writes nothing and deposes no one.
- **Listeners.** A cold listener's backlog is lost with its only holder. The no-loss promise of launch.md "Routing" holds across a child's end and a host restart only. The new launcher binds each group's registered address again as the host lists it. A bind refused because a survivor still holds the address makes that group failing, with the error, until the address is free.
- The host then reconciles as for a host restart, with an empty inventory: every `Dirty` row is a probe.

**Genesis (v2.32).** A group added for its genesis has activation 0 in its `groups` row. Its first serving start selects `Current(0)`, installs the store's first activation `v` under `LOCK` (§12.1 `Activating`), and the host records `v` with the store ID when it applies that start's `PlaceOpened` (above). A crash in between, whether the child's, the host's or the launcher's, leaves the store at `v` and the group at 0. Every later start at 0 is then refused `NotCurrent(Active(v))`, which the child reports as `PlaceCurrent(v)`. The host handles that answer as follows:
- **Recorded activation 0, no cutover.** The host records `v` for the group in one transaction that re-checks the incarnation, the recorded 0 and that the group is not retiring. The child's end then relaunches the group at `gen + 1` with `v` at once, outside backoff, as an Undecided probe's answer does.
- **The proof.** That start compares the store's `Describe` with its own before handles (§12.1), and only its `PlaceOpened` records the store ID. So a store of the same image opens and serves. A store that another image activated refuses this start too.
- **Any other `PlaceCurrent`** (the recorded activation is not 0, or the answer names 0) changes nothing, except a read reported by a probe of an Undecided deploy, or by a start of a group with a no-record mark, which §14.3 applies ("The probe's read", "Leaving no record"). The start's end is unsuccessful. The start's own cause compares the refusal's activation v with the activation w **this start was launched to open**, and names the record it ran, both from the start's own launch (for a probe of an Undecided deploy, the probe intent in the pending record; otherwise the selection the host launched that generation with, which after a host restart is the selection the inventory lists with the child, v2.41), never from the group's recorded pair (v2.40, astra m2-v252-astra-1965 item 3): `NotCurrent: the store is at activation v; this start requested w` when they differ, and `NotCurrent: the store's release identity at activation v is not that of assembly <hex>, which this start ran` when they are equal, since an equal activation is refused only for its identity digest (v2.42). So a probe of NEW at `Current(10)` refused at 3 whose read failed says it requested 10 and found 3, though the group still records 3. What `Status` shows as the group's `last` is set once, in "Failing and backoff" below: an `Unmatched` mark's cause comes first.

The genesis adoption is one-shot: a group with a recorded activation never adopts from 0 again, so the relaunch outside backoff happens at most once per incarnation. A host restart after the adoption's commit finds the activation already recorded and starts at `v`.

The window's crash points and the row after each, with no operator or deploy input:
- Dies before the install: the store is empty, and the probe at 0 runs genesis.
- Dies after the install and before `PlaceOpened` is applied (with or without a host restart): the probe at 0 is answered `PlaceCurrent(v)` and records `v`, and the next start at `v` opens.
- The host restarts after the adoption's commit and before it applies the probe's end: the restart's start is at `v`.
- Dies after `PlaceOpened` is applied: the probe is at `v`.

In each case the group serves again at `v`. No wake is lost: the row stays `Dirty` throughout, so it is probed. No start installs a second activation, because only a start at 0 on an empty store installs one. The generation fence and the clean-report rule are unchanged.

**Probes.** A probe is started without a capacity check, since its row is already counted. It recovers the store, arms its triggers at open (one already past due runs at once), runs until it is idle, and writes `Clean` by the clean report. By the invariant, a restart probes at most `max_places` places, and it never scans `Clean` rows.

**Failing and backoff (v2.27).** A start is **unsuccessful** when it ends with a `StartError`, is refused `Held`, or its child ends without a clean report before it has been `Ready` for 5 min (a crash, a kill, `Forced`, an exit with no report). The first unsuccessful start is retried at once; each further one waits a doubling delay from 1 s up to 5 min. A clean report, or 5 min in `Ready`, resets the count. While a retry waits, the group is **failing**: its row stays `Dirty(g)`, so it keeps its slot, its arrivals and due times are coalesced into the scheduled retry, and the host's status lists it with the last error, `Status.failing[].last`. This is the one statement of that field (v2.40, astra m2-v252-astra-1965 item 2), formatted when `Status` is answered, never stored by an end: for a group whose `recovery` mark is `Unmatched`, it is the mark's cause (§14.3 "What serves"), followed by `; last start: ` and the last start's own outcome; otherwise it is that outcome alone. A start's own outcome is its refusal when it gave one ("Genesis" above: `NotCurrent…`, `Held`, `PlaceStoreMismatch`, a bind error), including a refusal recovered with its end from the launcher's inventory (the last answer, "The control session" above), and otherwise the child's exit status, written `exit status <n>` for an exit and `killed by signal <n>` for a signal (v2.41), including an end with no report and an end the host recovered from the launcher's inventory after a restart. So no way a retry ends, and no host restart, hides the mark's cause while the mark holds it; an `Adopted` mark has none, so a relaunch that fails after an adoption shows only its own outcome. After `Forced` its store is at a crash cut, and the next open recovers it (§12.1 "At D"). An operator retry starts it now; a deploy, a retirement, or for a group with a no-record mark a retry whose read adopts a retained record (§14.3 "Leaving no record"), ends the failing state.

**Liveness.** A due trigger runs once its group can get a slot and a start succeeds. While every slot is held by live or failing groups, the no-eviction rule (§14.2) promises no firing time; the queued wake and its age are in the host's status, and the operator can raise `max_places` or retire a group.

**Removing a group (v2.27).** A group whose row is `Clean` is removed in one transaction, and the launcher then closes its listener. A group whose row is `Dirty`, failing or not, is **retired**:
1. The host commits a retiring mark in its `groups` row. From then on it launches nothing for the group and drops its wakes. A host restart that finds the mark goes on from step 2.
2. It asks the launcher `Retire(group, incarnation)`. The launcher refuses every later launch of that incarnation, stops each of its children, and answers `Retired` after all direct children have ended and all of the group's delegated cgroups are empty.
3. The host then removes the group's rows, lowers `dirty` and releases its tenant slot in one transaction, and records the store's disposition that the operator's request named: `keep`, where the store directory stays, detached from every group, and its pending triggers never run; or `delete`. The launcher closes the listener.

Retirement never writes `Clean` and runs no trigger. Until `Retired` is answered, the group keeps its slot.

### 14.6 Deploys and pending triggers

A deploy runs on a live place, so its row is `Dirty` for the whole deploy, NEW runs as a candidate at the next generation, and the row names NEW only once the cutover commits (§14.3).

**Claim authority for a Bucket-backed place (v2.90, STORE-SPEC §12.3 "ClaimAuth").** Every non-genesis objects@1 writer open needs a `store.ClaimAuth`, and the runner has one source for it:
- A managed launch (a wake or install child) takes the auth from its move envelope (codec `store.ClaimAuth`, launch words 25 and 26) and nothing else. Auth on the Options or the backing is dropped, so a managed launch with no envelope opens with none and is refused `Missing("claim_auth")` before any write. An envelope that does not decode, or that carries class `Adopt`, is refused the same way: Adopt is a local operator action on a non-Fleet namespace (fleet.md), never a move command. Only an embedding that is not a managed launch keeps the auth it put on its backing.
- An open refused `Superseded(actual)` ends the run `Deposed(actual)` and answers `PlaceDeposed(actual.epoch, actual.holder)`. Only `Superseded` maps to Deposed, because only it names the pair that deposed the place. A running writer's `Fenced(e)` names no holder and stays an ordinary error; the host's next launch with the same auth is then refused `Superseded(actual)` before any write and ends `Deposed(actual)`.
- The platform parks the assignment on `PlaceDeposed` (`platform-recovery`). No §14.5 restart follows, so a deposed place never duels its successor.
- `PlaceRead.claim` is `PlaceClaim { epoch, holder, attempt }`, the attempt taken from the head's ORIGIN (empty only at a genesis head, which has none). A host restarts the winner with `Continue(expect = (epoch, holder))`, or settles a lost-reply Permit with `store.settle_permit`. A claim or ORIGIN read that fails is returned as the read's error; only a backing with no head (`Missing("head")`) reports no claim.
- No production host launches a Bucket-backed place today (`Backing.Bucket` in `platform.local` is a separate item), so this changes no production behaviour now; it is the contract that item builds on.

**The prepare scan** is launch.md "Preparing the pending trigger report". It runs in the prepare child against the staged catalog, after `on_migrate` and `schema` (v2.45). `PrepareConfig` gains `triggers: triggers.Entries`, the same list the place passes in `TriggerConfig`. (v2.69) The prepare child runs `TriggerNoGrant`'s config check (§12.1) before the scan, so a listed granted entry always has an `attempt` mint and the scan's runnable class is the head's. The scan classifies each pending row with the head's own dispatch code, as the manifest preview uses `answer_want` (§12.2): `trigger_key` is the one function that says whether NEW has the row's entry, and `triggers.start`'s revision comparison says whether a started granted run is held. So the scan cannot disagree with what NEW's head does. Its classes are tried in `advance_trigger`'s order, and a row takes the first that applies (v2.27):
1. `cancelling`: its cancel is requested, parked or not. The head finishes a cancel at its due time before it looks at the entry.
2. `held`: a granted row that is parked, or whose attempt is at least 1 **and** whose revision stamp differs from NEW's identity digest (v2.42). `triggers.start` parks the second kind with a `Revision` hold before the head looks for the entry. A parked `Request` hold is held even when the digests are equal. A run that a release has already moved to NEW's stamp is not held. (v2.62) Each `held` row carries the fate the activation gives it, and the activation commit settles it ("The activation fate" below), so NEW's head never finds a `held` row parked by this activation.
3. `gone`: NEW's entry list does not allow its entry, or no registered entry of NEW has its kind, path and argument schema (`trigger_key`). A started granted run under an equal digest lands here when its entry is removed: `triggers.start` raises its attempt, and the head consumes it `Gone`.
4. `changed`: an allowed entry whose `revision` differs from OLD's.
5. `unchanged`: every other row.

Two records may share one identity (the same code under another backend, or a recipe that changed only grants), so NEW's identity digest can equal a started run's stamp; the scan compares the stamp, never assumes it differs. OLD's revisions come from the export's `current` activation row. The report is exact, and one that does not fit the prepare child's bound fails prepare with `Limit(triggers)`. (v2.62) `PrepareConfig` also gains `fates: Fates`, the deploy's fates. The scan resolves each `held` row's fate and writes the report's `fate` and `unmatched` fields. An `entries` element is **unmatched** when its `path` is the entry path of no pending row and of no entry OLD or NEW registers (v2.64), so a typo is refused, and a real path whose last held run ended between the operator's reading of a report and Begin, or during the drift retry, is vacuous. When every held row has a fate and no element of `entries` is unmatched, it stages one activation rewrite per held row with `stage_rewrite` (STORE §3.5a); a rewrite list that does not fit its frame fails prepare with `Limit(triggers)`. Otherwise it writes `TRIGGERS` and no `PREPARED`, and the host holds the request for the operator's decision (v2.81). (v2.82) The scan runs again on the final catch-up round's base inside the write pause; a held row that the request's fates do not settle aborts the export and holds the request. A standalone install runs the same classification and resolution on its live store under `LOCK`.

**The identity guard (v2.42, Spec Q5).** A granted trigger's revision stamp is the launch's identity digest (§6 "One identity"; `make_trigger_drive`), and a start of attempt ≥ 2 under another digest parks the run with a `Revision` hold. The identity covers `handles` (a table name, a binding, a key codec, an index extractor) through the entry's revision, which `Exported.revision` alone does not, so a started run never resumes against changed handles; and it excludes the backend, so a rebuild of the same code by another backend resumes the run instead of holding it. The prepare scan classifies the held runs as `held`, and the activation commit settles each one by its fate (v2.62, below). (Before v2.42 the stamp was the executable image digest, which held a run across a rebuild that changed nothing it reads.)

**The activation fate (v2.62, lane run-activation-fate-spec-2393; ENDSTATE `storage-runs`, steps law 9).** The activation that would hold a run also settles it. Every activation of a store that holds pending trigger rows, whether a hot deploy's prepared cutover or a standalone `--hirsel-install`<!-- doc-ref: external -->, decides the fate of each **held** row in the commit that activates NEW. No row stays parked because of an activation that has already happened. A held row is one the prepare scan classifies `held` against NEW's identity digest: one that is parked under any hold, or a granted row whose attempt is at least 1 and whose revision stamp differs from NEW's identity digest. The `cancelling` class comes first, so a row whose cancel is already requested is never held. The rule is the runner's and names no application: it reads only trigger rows and the identity digest.

- **The fate.** `HeldFate = Release | Cancel`. A deploy or install carries `Fates { all: Option[HeldFate], entries: List[EntryFate] }`, with `EntryFate { path: Bytes, fate: HeldFate }`. `entries` is sorted by `path` and has no duplicates. A held row takes the fate of the `entries` element whose `path` equals its entry path; otherwise it takes `all`. The fate is chosen per entry path, one fate for all `hold` groups of that path; `hold` informs the choice and never selects it (v2.66; "Preparing the pending trigger report"). It is never chosen per run.
- **What the activation commit writes.** Each held row is rewritten in place. The rewrite keeps its identity, its argument, its due time and its attempt counter, so the `due` index entry is unchanged.
  - `Release`: the state becomes `Waiting`, the revision stamp becomes NEW's identity digest, and `cancel` stays false. This is `triggers.release(id, Some(NEW digest))`, extended to a row that a cut attempt left `Running` or `Waiting` without a hold mark. The next start raises the attempt to n + 1 and runs NEW's code (steps law 9: "a release on the new code restamps the row").
  - `Cancel`: `cancel` becomes true and the state becomes `Waiting`. The stamp and the attempt stay. NEW's head finishes the row as `cancelling` at its due time, which has already passed for a started run: it commits the terminal `Cancelled` and deletes the row, exactly as for `triggers.request_cancel`.
  - Every other row is unchanged, byte for byte.
  - Under both fates the state is never `Running` after the commit, so no attempt is current. A late commit of the attempt that the activation cut is refused `StaleAttempt` by the fence (`current_attempt` requires `Running` and the same attempt). A late `trigger_boundary` or `trigger_snapshot` is refused the same way.
- **One commit.** The rewrites are rows of the activation cut itself, beside the catalog, the activation pair and the release row (§6). A reader of version v−1 sees every held row as it was, and a reader of v sees every held row settled, with nothing in between. A crash leaves one or the other (§6 "What a crash leaves"). The rewrites go through STORE's activation rewrites (STORE-SPEC v2.101 §3.5a "Activation rewrites"): on the prepared path they travel in PREPARED, computed by the prepare child against the exported base, which the cutover's drift check makes the base the cut applies to. On a standalone install the install stages them under `LOCK` on the live store with `store.stage_rewrite`. (v2.66) When NEW's migration replaces `store.triggers.pending`, the scan classifies the rows of the staged replacement range and the report is the scan's. The settle then calls `stage_rewrite` as for any table; on a table the staging replaces, `stage_rewrite` replaces the row in the staged range, and no 0x0085 rewrite is written for it (STORE v2.103 §3.5a; build slice F1.5). The attempt witness `prepared` (STORE §12.7) covers the rewrites frame, so whoever reads the activation reads which settlement it applied.
- **No default.** When an activation finds a held row whose fate is `None` (no matching `entries` element and `all` is `None`), or an `entries` element whose `path` is the entry path of no pending row and of no entry OLD or NEW registers (v2.64), the activation is refused **`Unsettled`** before it publishes any cut. The refusal carries the trigger report. A deploy's prepare child then writes no PREPARED, and the platform host holds the request for the operator's decision (v2.81, launch.md "Self-editing"): OLD's export is aborted once, as for L54's missing report, the decision's report shows each unfated `held` group and each unmatched path, and OLD keeps serving with its store unchanged. A standalone install exits 1 with the same report on stderr and publishes no cut. (On objects@1 its claim has already deposed a running writer, §6 "Genesis and explicit install".) An activation with no held rows needs no fate, and most deploys have none. There is no default, because each fate is a declaration only the operator can make:
  - Release declares the run's recorded steps valid on NEW's code, as a migration declares its rows valid. A silent default Release would return old step results to a reordered body, the wrong answer steps law 9's pair exists to stop.
  - Cancel ends work the program expected to finish. A silent default Cancel would do that on every code-changing deploy, because a drain cuts every in-flight run.
  - Leaving the run parked is the gap this rule closes.
- **Who supplies a fate.**
  - The operator supplies fates on a held deploy plan through `hirsel op decide` (either interactively or via `--decision FILE` naming `host_v1.Decision`), bound to the plan's exact `plan_digest` and held inventory (launch.md "The operator socket", "The operator CLI").
  - A standalone install takes it as `--hirsel-held=release|cancel`<!-- doc-ref: external --> and `--hirsel-held-entry=PATH=release|cancel`<!-- doc-ref: external -->, run `Args` like `--hirsel-install`<!-- doc-ref: external --> (launch.md "Place activation").
  - The operator's Decide on a hold whose report shows a held group or an unmatched path supplies the fates, which replace the request's recorded ones (v2.81); a Decide on any other hold keeps the Begin's fates. Only the operator decides (launch.md "Self-editing").
  - A Register is a genesis on an empty store and carries no fates.
  - An editor, and so a harness proposal's publisher, supplies no fates, as it supplies no Foreign or Exec approval (v2.81: its Begin is refused otherwise). A proposal deploy that finds a held run therefore holds in AwaitDecision, and the operator settles it with a Decide. Whether a run's history stays valid on new code is the operator's call, never the proposing agent's.
  - `hirsel dev` passes `Release` on every install it runs into the store it supervises, whether dev-owned or supplied with `--store`. (v2.74) The fate travels on dev's predecessor install launch, which the runner maps to `InstallFrom(Fates(all: Some(Release), entries: []))`. Dev passes no `--hirsel-held`<!-- doc-ref: external --> word and refuses one in the program arguments (launch.md "Dry run and dev loop").
  - In-process places (§14.9) have no deploy and activate only at genesis.
- **A run whose entry or step NEW no longer has.**
  - *Entry gone.* A released row whose entry NEW does not allow, or has no registered entry for (`trigger_key`), passes `triggers.start` under its new stamp. The head then consumes it with the terminal `Gone` at its next dispatch, and no body runs. A cancelled one ends `Cancelled`. Either way the row is consumed exactly once. The report shows it as `held`, with its fate.
  - *Step changed or gone.* A released run re-runs NEW's body from the top (steps law 1). At an index whose recorded fingerprint differs from NEW's request, law 4 holds it again with a `Request` hold, stamped with NEW's digest. A recorded step NEW's body never reaches stays as unread history, and the run's outcome commits as usual (steps law 6).
  - **Residual R-2393-1 (re-hold after release).** The re-hold in *Step changed or gone* comes from NEW's own code on this activation, not from the activation. The trigger report cannot predict it, because predicting it means running NEW's body. Steps law 4, as ratified, keeps it parked. Two things settle it: the next activation's fate, since the row is `held` in that report, or the program's own `triggers.release` or `triggers.request_cancel`. Changing law 4 so that a released run ends on a mismatch would overturn Sam's ruling, ruling 2 B' of 2026-11-23, so this spec does not make that change. (v2.64) **The operator's signal:** each `held` group of the trigger report is also keyed by `hold: Option[HeldBy]`, with `HeldBy = Cut | Revision(same_code: bool) | Request(same_code: bool)` (v2.66), which is `None` on every group of another class, like `fate`. `Cut` is a started run that is not parked, `Revision` and `Request` name the parking hold, and `same_code` is true when the rows' revision stamp equals NEW's identity digest. A `Request(same_code: true)` group was held by NEW's own code, so releasing it again on unchanged code re-runs the same body to the same request and law 4 holds it again. A `Revision(same_code: true)` group is a rollback to the code that started its runs, the one case where Release is exact. Law 4 and ruling 2 B' are unchanged.
- **What the store keeps.** A released row keeps its step rows and its step cap: the run's history is what release declares valid. A cancelled run's terminal commit deletes its trigger row and records `Cancelled` in `outcomes`. Its rows in `store.steps.rows` and `store.steps.caps` stay as the run's history, as after any terminal outcome. Its identity is never reused, so they are never read again as live state.
- **Replay.** The settlement is part of the activation checkpoint cut. A cut is not a journal frame and has no call record (§7; STORE §1.2 "Schema commits"). So `tape check` and re-execution never re-run it. A fork at any version from v onward has the settled rows as its base state, and a fork below v has them unsettled. Attempt n + 1 of a released run is an ordinary granted attempt: its transact calls are recorded with NEW's code bytes, so their recorded-code re-execution needs NEW's image, like any call of NEW. A recording spans one serving start, and a cutover ends OLD's start, so no tape spans an activation.

### 14.7 Laws

L40 runs in the runner's prepare harness and L44 in the runner's place harness, since each needs one place and no host. Every other law runs in a platform host harness (lane P3 in platform-host-spec-1741/REPORT.md) over places that use triggers, with the launcher's child launches faked only where the law says so. The oracle of each is the rule cited. L46's launcher half, where a child's end is sent after its listener's exit sample and before the watch resumes, and an inventory repeats a readable listener, is built in the launcher's own harness (crates/hirsel-core-driver/src/launcher/laws.rs, `end_samples_listener_before_watch`).

| Law | Wrong implementation it catches | Oracle |
|---|---|---|
| L39 `wake_due_after_unload`: a place commits a trigger due at t + 2 s and goes idle; its process ends with `Stopped(Some(t + 2000))`; with no arrival, the trigger's outcome is committed after wall time t + 2 s. (v2.27) While the wait is armed for a place due at t + 60 s, and again while the index is empty, a second place goes `Clean(Some(t + 2000))`; its trigger runs after t + 2 s, not at t + 60 s | the host keeps §13.5's no-wake rule, or writes `Clean(None)`, so the trigger never runs; or the wait is armed only at start, so an earlier deadline waits for the old one | §14.3, §14.4 |
