| L40 `trigger_scan_classes` (v2.27): **(a)** under a NEW image whose digest differs from OLD's, one OLD store holds a pending transact trigger for an entry NEW removes, a started granted run, a started granted run whose entry NEW removes, a cancelling row for an entry NEW removes, a parked row whose cancel is requested, one for an entry whose body NEW changes, and one for an unchanged entry; the scan reports `gone`, `held` (two), `cancelling` (two), `changed` and `unchanged`; with no fates the report is unsettled and no `PREPARED` is written; under (d)'s fates NEW's head consumes the first with `Gone`, finishes both cancels, runs the first started run's attempt 2 and finishes the second `Cancelled`. **(b)** Under a NEW recipe that differs in `n_place` only, so with an equal digest, and under `Fates(all: Some(Cancel), entries: [])`: a started granted run is `unchanged` and runs attempt 2; one whose entry NEW removes is `gone` and is consumed `Gone`; a run parked by a `Request` hold is `held` and needs a fate like any other, and this one ends `Cancelled`. **(c)** NEW lists no trigger entry at all: OLD's one future transact row is `gone`, and at its due time NEW's head, served by the normal serving path, commits its terminal `Gone`. **(d)** (v2.62; built in v2.72; tested on Cranelift and LLVM) (a)'s store under `Fates(all: Some(Release), entries: [EntryFate(path: <the second started run's path>, fate: Cancel)])`: the report's two `held` groups carry `Release` and `Cancel`, `unmatched` is empty, and `PREPARED` holds one activation rewrite per held row. (v2.64) (a)'s two `held` groups are keyed `hold` `Cut`, and (b)'s `Request`-parked run, under its equal digest, is a `held` group keyed `Request(same_code: true)`. ### 14.8 What this changes elsewhere, and the conflicts

| # | Existing rule | Conflict | Resolution |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | v2.21 §13.5 "the directory does not wake an unloaded place" | the platform must wake one | Resolved: the directory is deleted (row 9), and every hosted place, in-process or launched, is woken by §14. |
| 2 | v2.21 §13.7 Durable Objects "Not taken: alarm wake-up" | §14 takes it | Resolved: §14.10 takes it; §13.7 is deleted with the directory. |
| 3 | v2.21 §13.6 PD4 `dir_reopen_keeps` | none | Ported as `local_reopen_keeps` (§14.9), where the host's wake runs the due trigger. |
| 4 | §12.1 `Ended = Stopped \| Forced(u64)` | the clean report needs `next_due`, and a stop during `Starting` cannot know it | `Stopped(Option[u64]) \| Unstarted \| Forced(u64)`. The drain's finish returns `Stopped(next_due)`, a stop during `Starting` returns `Unstarted`, and `main` exits 0 for both but reports only `Stopped`. Every match on `Stopped` (the directory, the fixture, `main`) changes with it. Pre-1.0, nothing migrates. |
| 5 | §12.1 "operate publishes at each phase change"; the watch channel of capacity 6 | the idle stop adds inputs to `operate` | The idle stop adds messages on `operate`'s own inbox, not phases. No new phase, no new `Status` publish, and the watch channel stays at six sends. |
| 6 | §12.2 "the preview opens no store" | the trigger scan needs the store's rows | The scan runs in the prepare child, which already opens the export and stages the migration. The manifest preview is unchanged. |
| 7 | ENDSTATE `storage-triggers` "after a long outage every trigger … runs once at the next open" | none | A wake is an open. Lateness under `Full` is T2's lateness. |
| 8 | ENDSTATE `storage-runs` "a drain … is the same cut: the next start is attempt n + 1" | idle stops would cut runs | §14.4's idle rule counts active runs, so an idle stop never cuts one. An operator stop still does, as before. |
| 9 | v2.21 §13 directory (several places in one process) vs the authority group (one store, one process) | a directory runs several groups in one process | The directory is not a tenant mechanism. **Done (v2.31):** it is superseded by the platform host and deleted; PD1 to PD4 are ported to the host's in-process launcher (§14.9). |
| 10 | rule 11 "no guard against our own implementation" | the generation fence | The fence orders reports from processes that overlap during a cutover. |
| 11 | "no hidden cap changes an answer" (host rules) | the scan, the probe set and the queued wakes | The scan is exact or fails `Limit(triggers)`. Probes are all `Dirty` rows, which the invariant bounds, and nothing is truncated. A `Full` delay is reported in the host status and changes no trigger's result. |

### 14.9 In-process places (v2.31)

`local` (store/local.hirsel) is the platform host with an in-process launcher. A program that hosts first-party places in its own process, such as the harness gateway, uses it.

**Shape.** `local.local(config)` opens the host's catalog store at `root/host` (v2.85: with the session store's options and a `resident_limit` grown by `max_places` times the most one session adds to the catalog) and starts one actor that owns the `platform.Host` and every place task. The config names the place factory, the seed every start sees, `max_places`, the idle time each start gets, the wire sessions each place admits, the bytes each place may use and a watch channel. The actor answers the host's requests as a launcher would:
- `Hello` gets an inventory of the live place tasks; `Bind` is answered `Bound` with no listener (an in-process place takes its connections through `route`).
- `Start` of a serving child selects the place's backing from `Config.backing` (`DiskBacking` or `BucketBacking(LocalBucket)`): for `DiskBacking`, it makes the group's workdir under `root` and starts the factory's place on `Disk(root/workdir)`; for `BucketBacking`, it scopes the session prefix `<prefix>/<name>/` and starts the place on standalone `Bucket(BucketBacking)` when `Config.journal` is `None`, or on `StreamedBucket(BucketBacking, JournalSlot)` (allocated from the actor's one `store.journal_service` and opened via `store.streamed`, STORE-SPEC §12.11.1) when `Config.journal` is `Some(plan)`. A `DiskBacking` host with `Config.journal = Some(_)` is refused `Configuration` (STORE-SPEC §12.11.0). Every start opens at `Current(activation)`, as a hosted child selects (launch.md "Activation"), with the host's idle time as `PlaceConfig.idle` and its session count as `PlaceConfig.max_sessions`. A repeated `Start` of the same child answers `Launched`; a second child for a group, or any start while the host stops, is refused `Held`. (v2.73) A start whose charge (operate's bytes, the store share, `PLACE_KEEP` and the place task's own bytes) does not fit the room left in any shard of `place_bytes` (§14.11) is refused `Spawn("Full")`, and so is one whose place `operate`'s sizing refuses; no place task runs. A non-serving start is refused: an in-process host has no deploy.
- `Stop` and `Kill` both drain the place. A task cannot be killed; `operate` cuts a drain only at its own deadline.
- `Retire` drains the group's task, and `Close` of a group retired with `Delete` removes its workdir on a `DiskBacking` host. On a `BucketBacking` host it removes nothing: the session's objects under `<prefix>/<name>/` stay until an operator clears them, and no later session uses that prefix, because ids are never reused.
- **Bucket hosts.** A create seeds the session's store under `<prefix>/<name>/` and, unlike Disk, cannot clear it first. A seed that committed and then failed, or a host that crashed between the seed and the catalog commit, leaves a store past genesis (main above version 0, STORE-SPEC §12.3) under an id that was never listed. The next create finds that store, does not seed it again, and moves on to the next id; the catalog commit then records the higher next id, so the skipped id is never reused. Cold checks and cold reads (`check_cold`, `run_read`) open a session's Directory store and are refused `Configuration` on a `BucketBacking` host. `StreamedBucket(BucketBacking, JournalSlot)` is not the `Backing.*.journal` that STORE-SPEC §12.11.0 cuts for good: the Place gets a slot from the host's one service, never a `StreamPlan` of its own, so a Place cannot start a stream.
- Deploy, admission and collection requests are answered as by a launcher that holds none (`Fenced`, `Collected(0)`, `AdmissionFailed(Unassembled)`).

The in-process launcher starts groups from one linked factory. Its catalog has
no deploy admission or retained assembly images for an install child to select,
and its launch configuration states each place's allowance (§6 "One n_place").
It refuses an install launch's record selection as `Mismatch`. Genesis for
these groups runs on their first serving start at activation 0.

**Ends.** When the place reaches `Ready`, the actor checks its `Status.store_id` against the store the host recorded and reports `PlaceOpened`. A mismatch is reported `PlaceStoreMismatch` and the place is stopped. A start that fails `NotCurrent(Active)` is reported `PlaceCurrent`, with the probe's read when §14.3 asks for one. These places run on `Disk`, so that read is log@1's, under `LOCK`, and an in-process place starts no `FollowerService` (v2.54). When `operate` returns, the actor reports the end with the child's `gen`: `Stopped(next_due)` is exit 0 with the report, `Unstarted` is exit 0 with none, and anything else is exit 1. (v2.32) The routes waiting on a group whose start failed are refused only after the host has applied the end, and only if the host did not start the group again at once (§14.5 "Genesis", "Failing and backoff", and v2.39's adoption relaunch, §14.3 "Leaving no record"). So §14.4's clean-report rule applies unchanged. (v2.73) A serving start the launcher refused before a place task ran (above) follows the same rule: once the host has applied the refusal, and unless it started the group again at once, the routes waiting on the group are refused. A start refused for size, or one that ends `StartError::Full`, is answered `Full` (§14.2); any other refusal is `Failed` with its cause. A route that arrives while the group waits on backoff is answered by the next retry. The listener's readable bit is "a route waits for this group", so a route that arrives during a drain relaunches the place.

**Operations.** `add(name)` adds a trusted group with no listener and no limits; `remove(name)` retires it with `Delete` and answers after `Close`. `route(name)` wakes the group and answers with a lease once the host has applied its `PlaceOpened` and the place still serves. (v2.83) The lease holds the place's `Status` and its dial handle (§14.11); `peek` answers only a place that is already up and otherwise `Busy`. A lease does not hold the place up: the place's own §14.4 idle check, which counts sessions, decides. `unload(name)` is `platform.unload` and answers with the end. `stop` drains every place and closes the host.

**Fate-sharing.** In-process places share the host's process, so a fault in one ends all. This is allowed only for first-party code: every group `local` adds is trusted, and a tenant group runs only through a process launcher (§14.8 row 9). (v2.83) What a co-located place can and cannot reach of another is in §14.11 "Co-located places".

**Laws (ported from PD1 to PD4).**

| Law | Wrong implementation it catches | Oracle |
|---|---|---|
| `local_one_head`: concurrent first routes to one cold group give one child; both are answered by it | each route that finds no ready child starts one, so two heads run and the second cannot open the store | §14.3 |
| `local_route_during_unload`: a route during an unload waits for the drain and is answered by a fresh child | the route is answered with the draining child | §14.3, §14.4 "Unload" |
| `local_idle_unload`: an idle child stops by `operate`'s drain, ends `Stopped`, and its group is routed again only after the store is closed | the launcher reports the end when it asks the child to stop, so the next child finds the store held | §14.4 |
| `local_reopen_keeps`: a group routed again after an unload keeps its committed writes, and a trigger that came due while it was unloaded runs | the next child starts on a fresh store or a fresh activation | §14.1, §14.4 |
| `local_start_full` (v2.73): a host whose `place_bytes` (1 MiB) is below one child's charge is routed to its one group, so every start of the group is refused for size. The route is answered `Full`, and no child reaches Ready | the refusal is reported to the host, but the route waits for a child that never starts, or it is answered `Failed` | §14.2, §14.9 "Ends" |
| `local_creates_to_max_places` (v2.85): a host whose session stores have a 1 MiB `resident_limit` creates `max_places` (256) sessions, each with a key at `store.ROW_KEY_LIMIT` less 2 bytes. Every create answers its id, and the listing holds all 256 | the catalog is opened with the session store's options and fills near the 120th create, or the refusal is reported as `Failed("commit")` | §14.9 "Shape" |
| `local_genesis_recovers` (v2.32): a group's store was activated by its genesis (version 1), and the host's catalog holds no activation for it. A task cannot be killed in-process, so the law builds the crash's durable state: it removes `root/host` and adds the group again. The next route is answered by a Ready child at activation 1 | the start at 0 is refused `NotCurrent(Active(1))`, the route is refused before the host acts, or the group never records 1, so it never serves without an operator | §14.5 "Genesis" |

### 14.10 Prior art (each source opened)

- **Cloudflare workerd** (e9a7b9c, src/workerd/server/alarm-scheduler.c++). One alarm row per actor, `_cf_ALARM(actor_id, scheduled_time)` (:55-60). At startup it loads every row into memory and arms a timer for each (:81-99). An alarm runs only once the clock reads at least its time, and it re-waits if woken early (:180-192). A failed alarm is retried with backoff, up to `RETRY_MAX_TRIES` (alarm-scheduler.h:54-66, .c++:247-263). **ADOPTED:** one durable due-time row per place, and the clock re-check. **ADAPTED:** a row is `Clean(next_due)` only after a clean stop, and the scan at startup reads only `Dirty` rows, bounded by `max_places`, where workerd holds every alarm in memory (its own TODO, :84-85). **NOT TAKEN:** engine retries. A Hirsel trigger's failure is recorded and consumed, and re-arming is the program's choice (ENDSTATE triggers).
- **Rivet** (de28d20, rivetkit-rust/packages/rivetkit-core/src/actor/). The actor pushes its next alarm to the engine before it sleeps (schedule.rs:902-916, :956-961). A new generation pushes again, and a stale alarm from an earlier generation "wakes the actor once with nothing due; it never drops a pending alarm" (schedule.rs:918-926). An actor may sleep only with no run handler, no connection and no HTTP request (sleep.rs:218-251). **ADOPTED:** sleep only when no run or connection is active (§14.4), and a generation on each start. **REPLACED:** Rivet tolerates a stale alarm, while §14 fences reports by generation and writes `Clean` only from a clean stop, so a crash leaves `Dirty` and a probe, never a lost wake.
- **Goose** (d5ae5bb). A single-process language with no durable scheduler, no places and no process host. **NOT TAKEN:** nothing to take.
- **The activation fate (v2.62), Goose** (d5ae5bb). Goose has no durable runs and no deploys, so there is nothing to settle. **NOT TAKEN.**
- **The activation fate, Restate** (7f97e97). Each invocation is pinned to the deployment that started it (`pinned_deployment`, crates/storage-query-datafusion/src/invocation_status/row.rs:153). An operator moves one paused invocation to another deployment through a per-invocation resume request, `ResumeInvocationRequest.update_pinned_deployment_id` (crates/types/src/invocation/mod.rs:1160-1178), which `restart_as_new` applies (crates/worker/src/partition/state_machine/lifecycle/restart_as_new.rs:237-242). **ADAPTED:** the pin is the revision stamp, and moving it is `Release`. **REPLACED:** the per-run operator request (option C in platform-audit REPORT "Decision for the lead"). Restate keeps old deployments registered, so a pinned invocation can wait for one. A Hirsel place serves one revision, so nothing could ever run a run left pinned to OLD. Its fate is decided where OLD stops being runnable, in the activation commit. The weakness taken into account: Restate's per-invocation move leaves the operator to find every pinned invocation. The trigger report finds them here.
- **The activation fate, Golem** (81cb597). A worker update is `Automatic` (replay the oplog on the new component) or snapshot-based (`PendingUpdateKind`, golem-common/src/model/agent/status.rs:1530). A replay that diverges fails the update (golem-worker-executor/src/durable_host/recovery.rs:295-325). **ADAPTED:** `Release` is the automatic update. A divergence is steps law 4's re-hold (residual R-2393-1). **NOT TAKEN:** reverting to the old version after a divergence, because OLD's code is not kept running; and snapshot updates, because a run's steps are its history and there is no user snapshot hook.

### 14.11 Places on every core, reached without sockets (v2.83)

§14.9 runs every place as a task in the actor's own unit, and each place binds a loopback listener. This section moves places onto helper units and replaces the listener with a dial handle. What §14.9 says about the host, its catalog, starts, ends, operations and laws still holds. The changes are described here.

**Shards.** `Config.units: u64` names how many helper units (CONC-15) `local` starts. `local` refuses a value of 0 with `Configuration`. The actor makes all of them at `local.local` and runs no place itself, so one unit and eight units behave the same way. A shard is the root task of one helper. It receives start jobs over a connection (CONC-17), spawns each place as a task in its own unit, and reports each end over a second connection.
- *Pool size.* The pool size is a Config field. It is not derived from cores. Hirsel has no core-count primitive, and CONC-15 keeps the worker count unobservable: a program that sized itself from cores would run differently on each machine. The host program chooses `units`. The harness gateway passes its operator's setting.
- *Placement.* Each start goes to the shard with the most room (its share less the charges of its live places). A tie goes to the lowest index. A start is refused `Full` exactly when its charge exceeds the most room any one shard has. This is §14.9's `Full` rule applied to each shard: the charge is unchanged, and the bound is the share.
- *Not sticky.* A place stays on its shard until it ends, and every start places it again. A woken place reopens its store from disk (§14.4). No memory survives a sleep, so returning to the same shard keeps nothing. A sticky rule would only send a busy place back to a full shard.
- *Charging.* Each shard admits place charges (§14.9 "Shape") against `place_bytes / units`. The remainder is unused. `host_bytes` also funds each helper's control allowance, including its task handles, registration and connections. The allowance is outside the place share because the runtime charges the helper's root task for these allocations.
- `/wholehog`: the end state is all places on helper units, with one rule for every unit count. A "units = 1 means the actor's own unit" path would be a second runtime shape for the same config. A derived pool size would make the program depend on the machine it runs on. Both are NOT TAKEN.

**What crosses.** A unit boundary carries only `cross` values (CONC-2).
- A start job carries the group's name and an actor-assigned job id, the workdir's narrowed `Dir` capability, plain overrides, the activation, the idle time and the session count. The actor binds the job id to the group and `gen`. A shard opens its start inbox and returns its client during initialization because an inbox stays in the unit that created it.
- An admission reply carries the stop client and the exact charge. The Ready event carries the dialer. Every status publication carries a shared, cross image with phase, digest, target, place allowance, release, activation, store identity, retention floor, address and upkeep counts. The image carries the typed encoding of store attention, including index state, drift and reclaim counters. `List[IndexReport]` cannot cross because its elements contain owned strings. The actor reconstructs the status in its unit and publishes it to the lease's `Latest[runner.Status]`. The end event carries the full runner result, with the install hold report encoded for the same list restriction.
- The actor stops a place by dropping its stop client. The place's watcher sees `Closed` and drains. A shard joins an ended place task before it sends the end event that refunds the charge. The actor dispatches one undecided start at a time so each placement uses settled charges. It can route already-ready places and process ends while that shard evaluates the start.
- The store, its database, grants, `Sender`s, `Latest`s and closures never cross. Each place opens its own store inside its shard. The actor's catalog store stays in the actor's unit.
- `seeded_factory` takes a plain `make: fn(take Supplied, T) -> Result[PlaceConfig[G,H,S], Capacity]` and `copy_seed: fn(T) -> T`, with `T: cross`. The opaque `Factory[T]` keeps the typed dispatch in the actor. Before spawning each shard the actor calls `copy_seed` once and passes the result and the plain maker to the shard's entry. Registrations stay on the unit that builds them. An unseeded `factory` has seed `()`. A seed can carry connection clients, `Shared` values and capabilities. The same-unit control `Door` carries requests with closures and leases. A place that needs gateway operations receives a connection client for those requests from its host program.

**Transport.** `PlaceConfig.listen` is `Listen = Tcp(Addr) | InProcess(Inbox[std.wsock.Dial])`. `Tcp` binds as before. `InProcess` binds nothing and does not report `Listening`. It hands `operate` an inbox of dials. `std.wsock.dials(n)` makes the connection and its `Dialer`, which is `cross`. `local` makes it when it sizes the place, before `operate_bytes`, so the place's reservation counts each session's dial room, and a lease carries a copy of the `Dialer` once the place is Ready (`local.dialer(lease)`; a peek of a child not yet Ready has none).
- (v2.84) A dial is `Dial { back: Client[Bytes], accept: Client[Accepted] }`, and `Accepted { code: u16, to: Client[Bytes] }`. Each direction carries WebSocket frame bytes exactly as a socket would, masked from the client, so the frame codec, `max_frame` and close frames are the socket path's. Each direction holds 16 items of at most `max_frame + 14` bytes. `std.wsock`'s reader refill and writer put are the only transport branches. The client makes its own receive connection and sends its client as `back`; `std.wsock.local_client` sends the dial and waits for `accept`.
- `serve_sockets` takes a dial where it takes an accepted socket. The dial passes through the same steps as a socket: the session count against `max_sessions`, admission while draining (`closing_at`), and the owner. (v2.84) A refused dial is answered with the code transport admission answers a socket, HTTP 503, whether the place is full or draining, and the dialer sees `Http(503)`. An admitted dial gets a wire owner, and `std.wsock.local_server` makes the owner's receive connection and sends it back as `Accepted(101, ..)`. Dials still queued when the place stops are dropped, so their dialers read `Eof` and route again.
- The wire owner is the same code. The transport sits below it and only moves messages. The call credit (§3 "Call credit", the `Credit` epoch fence included), the Hello deadline and its binding, `c.socket.max_frame` (1009), auth expiry, the caller checks, backpressure and the close codes all run unchanged on both transports. (v2.84) A `conn` receive has no deadline, so the reader's idle probing and the 30 s message deadline are TCP-only; an in-process peer that stops sending is ended by the owner's alarms and by its own close, and a dialing place's idle vote has no accept loop to park. An in-process session's source address is `127.0.0.1`, so no binding gives it more trust than a local TCP client.
- `/hirsel/status` stays HTTP-only. An in-process reader reads the lease's `Status`.
- A dial sent after the place has stopped answers `Closed`, because the place's dial inbox has been cleaned. The caller routes again, just as a TCP caller does after connect is refused.
- Decision: a host-level transport, not a lazy per-place listener. A lazy listener would still cost one listener, one accept task and its FDs for every place that is reached, which is the cost this section removes. It would also keep two ways for first-party code to reach a place. `/wholehog`: one transport inside the process and one front at its edge.

**External access.** A host program reaches the outside through its own front, one listener per host. The harness gateway's HTTP front, `gateway_http`, is that front. It reaches places by routing and dialing. `local`'s places have no listener of their own. A hosted child of a process launcher keeps the listener it inherits (§14.1), because it is a separate process.

**Co-located places.** A unit is a boundary for scheduling and accounting. It is not a trust boundary. Capabilities are the boundary, and every place `local` runs is first-party (§14.9 "Fate-sharing").

| Surface | What stops it | What does not |
|---|---|---|
| Another place's store | Each place holds only its own workdir's `Dir`, narrowed at the start job, and opens its own store. No path from one place's capabilities reaches another's workdir. | Code with the host's root `Dir` (the host program itself) |
| Another place's sessions | A dial client comes only from `route`, through the host. A place given the route `Door` (the gateway's subagents) still faces the target's Hello binding and its caller checks, exactly as over TCP. | — |
| A stale handle | A lease and its dial belong to one `gen`. After an unload the dial answers `Closed`, so it never reaches the next child (`local_stale_dial`). | — |
| Memory | Each shard's share and each place's admitted charge bound it. One place cannot use more than its shard's share. | A place can fill its own shard's share and refuse starts on that shard. |
| CPU | Places on different shards run in parallel. | A place that loops without waiting starves the places on its shard (a first-party denial of service, as in §14.9). |
| The seed | Each shard receives its own copy. | A `Shared` value or client in the seed is visible to every place. That is the seed's purpose. |
| Timing | — | Places on one shard share a scheduler and caches. Timing side channels exist, as between any first-party tasks. |
| A fault | — | A fault in any place ends the process (§14.9 "Fate-sharing"). |

*Self-check.* Run as an attacker with one place's code, it can reach another place only through a capability it was given (the route `Door`), and through that capability only by authenticating as any external client must. It can exhaust only its own shard's share and its own shard's CPU. It learns nothing from a `Status` it cannot route to. The residual risks are timing and denial of service within a shard. Both are first-party only, and a tenant runs through a process launcher (§14.8 row 9).

**Laws.**

| Law | Wrong implementation it catches | Oracle |
|---|---|---|
| `local_dial_parity` (runner-dial-law.hirsel): bind_granted's place runs under the fixture's `start` (TCP) and `start_dialed` (in process). On each, credential [2] binds Caller(2, [2]); with both of `max_sessions` 2 held, a third is refused 503; credential [2, 2] closes 1008 | The dial path skips a check the socket path makes: it admits past the limit, or its Hello skips the binding | Hand-derived from §3 "Binding" and §9 "excess sessions", the same for both arms |
| `local_stale_dial`: a lease from `gen` g is unloaded, and the group is routed again (`gen` g+1, possibly on another shard). A dial through the old lease answers `Closed` and never reaches the new place | Dial clients are kept per group rather than per child, so an old lease reaches the next child or another place on the same shard | §14.3's generation fence |
| `local_units_run_together`: the host routes two places. A requests a token without waiting and polls for it before its first wait. B answers 41. A returns 42 only if it saw the token while it was polling. | Places share one unit, so B cannot run until A waits | CONC-15 runs one task at a time between waits in each unit. |
| `local_shard_full`: with two 64 MiB shares, a place that costs more than 64 MiB and at most 127 MiB is refused `Full`. A smaller place fits. | The actor admits from the aggregate 128 MiB pool | The large place adds 63 MiB of publisher reserve, and the admitted small place costs over 1 MiB. |
| `local_units_zero`: configuring zero helper units immediately returns `Configuration`. | A zero-unit host starts or reports a capacity refusal | §14.11 configuration rule. |

**Slices.** (1) The transport, `Listen` and `local_dial_parity`. (2) Shards, `Factory` and seed changes, `local_stale_dial` and `local_units_run_together`. (3) Deleting the per-place listener path from `local`. Each slice lands as it qualifies. The report for lane platform-local-scale-2815 records which slices have landed.

**Prior art.**
- **Rivet** (de28d20, rivetkit-rust/packages/rivetkit-core). One process hosts many actors and takes all of their traffic through one engine connection. The registry dispatches each request to its actor in process as a `DispatchCommand` carrying encoded bytes (actor/task.rs:178-203, registry/dispatch.rs:16). **ADOPTED:** one front per process, and in-process dispatch of encoded payloads. **ADAPTED:** Rivet's actors share one Tokio runtime. Hirsel places run on helper units that share no memory, so a place reaches another only through a capability. **NOT TAKEN:** Rivet's per-actor HTTP surface; `/hirsel/status` stays on the front.
- **Goose** (d5ae5bb). It has no places and no host. **NOT TAKEN:** nothing to take.

### 14.12 Fleet seams on the host (lane platform-fleet-seams-3081)

The host's side of the fleet contract in [fleet.md](../../../docs/spec/fleet.md): who this host is, which work fleet@1 has assigned it, and what it has reserved for incoming moves. Every rule here is checked in the host's catalog transaction, against durable rows, so a restart neither forgets nor repeats a decision.

#### 14.12.1 Node identity and incarnation

- **Identity.** A host's node identity is the `NodeId` (`u64`) that fleet@1 enrolled it as. That is the value the move kernel fences on (`PermitRow.target_node`, `core/programs/fleet/types.hirsel`). `enroll(h, node)` records it in the catalog's `platform.node` row once, for the life of the catalog. The same id again is a no-op, a different id is `Denied`, and `0` is `Missing("node")`.
- **Incarnation.** Every open of the catalog commits `incarnation + 1` (starting at 1) in the same transaction that creates the catalog's other rows, before the host sends `Hello`. `incarnation(h)` reads it, and it is fixed for the life of the process. This is the single incarnation that fleet.md FLT-3 records as durable fleet@1 state, and the value `PermitRow.target_incarnation` names: the host mints it, and fleet@1 records what hosts report. Hirsel has no second notion of incarnation. It is unrelated to a group's incarnation (`launcher.Group.incarnation`, `Meta.next_incarnation`), which counts registrations of one group name.
- **Never repeated.** A host that lost its catalog opens unenrolled at incarnation 1. fleet@1 never reuses a `NodeId` (fleet.md FLT-3), so it enrolls the host under a fresh one. The pair `(node, incarnation)` therefore never names two processes, and anything signed for an earlier process is refused by the next one (§14.12.2).
- **Law** `node_incarnation` (§14.7).

#### 14.12.2 Signed assignments, a wake source

Besides an arrival, a due time and an operator wake (§14.3), fleet@1 wakes a place by **assigning** its group to this node: `assign(h, Assignment { group, node, incarnation, generation }, sig, key)`. `key` is the fleet's pinned Ed25519 public key, which the caller holds.

- **Signed bytes.** fleet@1 signs `assignment_message(a)`: the ASCII context `"hirsel platform assignment v1"`, one zero byte, then `node`, `incarnation` and `generation` as u64 little-endian, the group name's byte length as u64 little-endian, and the name's UTF-8 bytes. Because of the context, a signature over any other fleet message (an approval, a keyset) cannot verify as an assignment. Verification is `ed25519.verify` (core.md "Signature verification"). A key that is not 32 bytes, a signature that is not 64 bytes, or a failed verification is `Denied`.
- **Bound to this process.** `node` must be the host's enrolled `NodeId`, or the assignment is `Denied`. `incarnation` must be the current one (§14.12.1), or it is `WrongIncarnation(current)`. So no assignment signed for an earlier process of this node is ever accepted, including one replayed after a restart.
- **Generation floor.** The catalog's `platform.assignments` row holds each group's last accepted `(incarnation, generation)`. A lower generation is `Stale(recorded)`. The same generation is a no-op: it does not wake the place again. A higher one replaces the row.
- **One transaction.** The row, the group check (`Unknown`, `Retiring`) and the turn of a `Clean` wake row to `Dirty` under §14.2's capacity and quota checks all commit together. A full host commits none of them and answers `Full`, so fleet@1 sees the refusal and the generation is not consumed. A group that is already `Dirty` records the row and starts nothing: its authoritative child, or the probe that recovers it (§14.5), runs the place. Because the wake is the durable `Dirty` row, a restart between the commit and the launch finds the place `Dirty` and probes it. Nothing is lost and nothing is queued in memory.
- **Law** `assignment_wake` (§14.7).

#### 14.12.3 Destination reservations

Before a move's permit is consumed, the destination holds room for it (fleet.md FLT-4): `reserve(h, Reservation { group, node, incarnation, generation, capacity }, sig, key)`. `capacity` counts §14.2's place slots, so reservations and running places are one ledger, not two.

- **Signed and bound** as an assignment is (§14.12.2), over `reservation_message(r)`. That is the context `"hirsel platform reservation v1"`, one zero byte, then `node`, `incarnation`, `generation` and `capacity` as u64 little-endian, the group name's byte length as u64 little-endian, and the name's UTF-8 bytes. The node, the incarnation (`WrongIncarnation`) and the signature are checked as §14.12.2 checks them.
- **One row per group.** `platform.reservations` holds a group's latest reservation. A higher generation replaces the earlier one, and its slots are counted once, so a move retried under a new generation never holds its slots twice. The same generation again is a no-op. A lower generation, or any generation at or below the group's last assignment, is `Stale(recorded)`. Capacity 0 holds a generation and no slots.
- **The capacity rule.** A reservation is accepted when `dirty` + the slots of every other group's reservation + `capacity` ≤ `max_places`; otherwise it is `Full(Count(max_places))`. §14.2's check before `Dirty` becomes `dirty` + the slots reserved by other groups ≥ `max_places` ⇒ `Full`. A group's own reservation does not count against its own start. Lowering `max_places` below `dirty` + all reserved slots is `Busy`. The invariant `dirty` + reserved ≤ `max_places` therefore holds in every committed state. The sum is read from the rows each time and never stored: reservations are the few moves in flight to one node.
- **Ends.** A reservation ends when the group's assignment at that generation or later commits, in the same transaction as the wake (§14.12.2), or when this incarnation ends: every open of the catalog deletes all reservations in the transaction that raises the incarnation (§14.12.1). fleet@1 learns of the second case from `WrongIncarnation` and reserves again on the new incarnation.
- **Law** `reservation_generations` (§14.7).

#### 14.12.4 Code objects fetched by hash

A fleet host has no operator upload. It answers the launcher's `Need` from the fleet's bucket, through the launcher's existing admission exchange (launch.md "Admission"): `Admit`, then `Need`, then `AdmitData`, then `AdmitEnd`. Nothing in the launcher or its wire changes.

- **Layout.** Under the bucket's code prefix, the object a `Wanted` key names is `objects/<kind>/<hash>`. `<kind>` is `group`, `body`, `annex`, `archive` or `value`, and `<hash>` is the key's 32-byte hash in lowercase hex. Only the operator or publisher writes this prefix (fleet.md FLT-6). `code_object_key(w)` computes the name.
- **Frames.** `fetch_need(client, attempt, keys, last, max_object, deadline)` GETs every key of one `Need` part. An object over `max_object` bytes fails the GET with the bucket's `TooLarge`; the caller sets the bound, since the launcher's own intake limits apply again on admission. It answers them in one `AdmitData` holding, for each key in order, the launcher's object frame: the tag (1 group, 2 body, 3 annex, 4 archive, 5 value), the 32-byte hash, the length as u64 little-endian, and the bytes. After the part marked last, and only then, it appends the zero tag that ends the frames and sends `AdmitEnd`.
- **Verification.** The host does not hash what it fetched. The launcher hashes every object and refuses one whose bytes do not match its key (`Hash(h)`), so a wrong or tampered bucket object fails admission. Which code may run is decided by the operator-signed approval of the recipe root (fleet.md FLT-6), not by the bucket.
- **Refusals.** A source blob (`BlobKey`) or a hash that is not 32 bytes is `Unfetchable`. A failed GET, `NotFound` included, is `Bucket(key, error)`. In either case nothing is sent for that part, and the caller releases the attempt.
- Which admissions are answered from the bucket, rather than by an operator connection, is the fleet deploy's choice (fleet.md Phase 3). This section gives it the feed.
- **Law** `bucket_fetch_need` (§14.7).

## 15. JWT bindings (new in v2.60; Runner D4)

A place that accepts JWTs binds its sessions with a library binding, `jwtbind`, over `std.jwt` (`core/std/jwt.hirsel`, contract `core/std/api/jwt.md`). `jwtbind` is ordinary application-level code in its own package (planned: package `jwtbind` under core/programs/jwtbind, binding `runner` and `std`). It is a `Wire.bind` like any other: the runner calls it, bounds it and trusts its answer (§3 "Binding"), and still reads no credential itself (rule 13). Nothing in `jwtbind` names a product, an identity provider or a claim beyond RFC 7519's registered claims. This section gives only the delta. The binding contract, `BoundCaller`, expiry enforcement and its 4401 close (CHANGES-v2.57, L62), the deletion of `anon:` minting (CHANGES-v2.24 item 3) and the SDK's credential recovery (docs/spec/sdk.md, "A place's binding may give the session an expiry") are unchanged except where §15.6 says so.

Constants marked GUESS have no measured part. Slice J4 measured `BIND_STEPS` and `BIND_BYTES` (§15.2).

### 15.1 Two runner changes, both generic

**(a) The binding's key set and sealed keys come in its `G` (v2.70; v2.60 to v2.69 had a binding seed).** A binding that verifies signatures needs a key set that changes while the place runs. A binding keyed by a `Secret` needs a holder of that key. v2.60 gave `runner.wire_bind` a `Latest[B]` seed. That serves the key set but cannot serve the key, because no `Latest` can hold a resource (A7). v2.70 deletes the seed. The binding takes the place's `G`, minted for each bind by `Grants.bind` (§12.1), just as a claim function takes the `claim` mint's `G` (http.md K-2):

```
pub fn wire[G: send](bind: StaticFn[fn(G, Bytes) -> Result[BoundCaller, BindRefusal] waits], bind_bytes: u64, bind_steps: u64) -> Wire[G]
```

- **What the mint copies.** The mint puts into each `G` what the binding needs, and none of these copies waits:
  - a `latest.handle` of the application's `Latest` for a changing key set;
  - a `secret.share` holder for a sealed key;
  - a copy for a plain value.
- **One version per bind.** The binding calls `latest.read` once per bind, so a bind sees exactly one published version for its whole run. The application keeps a sibling handle and publishes new values with `latest.publish`. A publish never changes what a running bind holds (CONC-13).
- **No `G` after Welcome.** The `G` is dropped when the bind ends, and a session keeps only its Caller.
- **What is built.** The built `runner.wire(seed: B plain, ..)` copies a plain seed, and `runner.wire_bind(take seed: Latest[B], ..)` reads a published one. Both retire in slice J7.

This is not D9 (c). D9 (c) dropped a shared `Arc[G]` as an entry's authority, and R6 requires each use to get its own `G`. A bind's `G` is minted for that one bind and reaches only the binding (rule 13), never an entry or a Caller. A `Latest` inside it carries public data only (A7).

**(b) A binding can say "not now".** A binding's error was `()`, so a binding that cannot verify because its key set is unavailable had to refuse the credential. The SDK then calls the credential source, gets the same bytes, and ends in `Unbound` (docs/spec/sdk.md). One outage of a key server would strand every client. v2.60:

```
pub type BindRefusal: codec + send = Rejected | Unavailable
```

| Binding answer | WS, before Welcome | HTTP |
|---|---|---|
| `Ok(b)`, unexpired (CHANGES-v2.57) | Welcome | the request proceeds |
| `Error(Rejected)`, a fault, an exhausted bound, or an expired `b` | Close 1008 | 401 |
| `Error(Unavailable)` | Close 1013 | 503 with `Retry-After: 5` (GUESS) |

`Unavailable` tells a client only that the place cannot decide now. Beyond that it reveals at most whether a token's `kid` is in a public key set (§15.3), which is no oracle on the credential. A fault or an exhausted bound stays a rejection, because the binding is bounded application code and a fault inside it says nothing about the place's state. No outcome is recorded (unchanged).

### 15.2 Configuration

```
pub type Source: send = Static(String) | Remote(RemoteSource) | Mac(List[MacKey])   // v2.70: Mac
pub type MacKey: send { kid: String, key: Secret }   // v2.70: a sealed key whose only use is `verify` (TLS-5)
pub type RemoteSource: send { url: String, every_ms: u64, stale_ms: u64, fetch_ms: u64 }
pub type Issuer: shareable + send {
    iss: String,          // the exact "iss" string; required
    aud: String,          // this place's audience; required
    typ: Option[String],  // when Some, the header's typ must match (§15.3 step 3); default None
    issuer: u8,           // the Caller issuer number this iss maps to; 2..=255, from the place's fixed table
    allow: std.jwt.Allow, // EdDsa, Es256, Rs256 with Static or Remote; Hs256, Hs384, Hs512 with Mac (v2.70); never both
    leeway_s: u64,        // clock skew; 0..=MAX_LEEWAY_S, default 60
    max_token: u64,       // credential bytes before parsing; ..=std.jwt.MAX_TOKEN, default 8192
    max_life_s: u64       // how far ahead of the bind exp may lie; 1..=86400, default 3600 (both GUESS)
}
pub type Seed: shareable + send { issuer: Issuer, clock: Clock, keys: Option[std.jwt.PublicKeys], good_until_mono_ns: u64, confirmed_until_mono_ns: u64 }
pub fn verifier(issuer: Issuer, take source: Source, net: Net, take trust: Trust, clock: Clock, random: Random, tasks: Tasks) -> Result[Verifier, ConfigError] waits   // trust: the roots for the https fetch (std.http_fetch); random: the jitter
pub type Grant: send { seed: Latest[Seed], mac: List[MacKey] }   // v2.70: the jwtbind part of the place's G
pub fn grant(v: Verifier) -> Grant                               // v2.70: replaces seed(v); put it in the bind mint's seed
pub fn share(g: Grant) -> Grant                                  // v2.70: the bind mint's per-bind copy: latest.handle and secret.share; never waits
pub fn bind(g: Grant, credential: Bytes) -> Result[runner.BoundCaller, runner.BindRefusal]   // v2.70: does not wait (CHANGES-v2.70 F2)
pub const BIND_BYTES: u64 = 262144   // J4: 114,736 measured at max_token = MAX_TOKEN (65536); 14,384 at 8192
pub const BIND_STEPS: u64 = 1048576  // J4: 516,821 measured at max_token = MAX_TOKEN; 65,669 at 8192
pub const MAX_LEEWAY_S: u64 = 300
```

(v2.70) A place puts a `jwtbind.Grant` in its `G` and wires two one-line functions:

```hirsel unbuilt
fn mint_bind(s: Seed, _h: Handles, _i: ()) -> Result[G, Capacity] { Ok(G(jwt: jwtbind.share(s.jwt))) }
fn bind(g: G, credential: Bytes) -> Result[runner.BoundCaller, runner.BindRefusal] { jwtbind.bind(g.jwt, credential) }
// config.wire = Some(runner.wire(code.describe(bind), jwtbind.BIND_BYTES, jwtbind.BIND_STEPS))
// config.grants.bind = Some(runner.Mint(seed: Seed(jwt: jwtbind.grant(v)), mint: mint_bind, mint_bytes: .., mint_steps: ..))
```

Here `Seed` and `G` are the place's own types. A place whose binding and claim function share a principal calls `bind` from both.

`verifier` checks its configuration before it returns. Each of these is a `ConfigError`, so a misconfigured place does not start:
- an empty `iss` or `aud`;
- `issuer` 0 or 1, since 1 is anonymous and the runner refuses a bound issuer 1 anyway;
- an HMAC algorithm in `allow` with a `Static` or `Remote` source (`Hmac`), or an asymmetric one with `Mac` (`Asymmetric`, v2.70);
- (v2.70) a `Mac` list that is empty, holds more than 4 keys (GUESS), or has an empty or repeated `kid` (`MacKeys`);
- (v2.70) a `Mac` key that can make a tag or cannot verify (`KeyUse`): `secret.jws_hmac_sign(key, alg, b"")` must answer `Denied`, and `secret.jws_hmac_verify` must not;
- (v2.70) a `Mac` key below the minimum of an algorithm in `allow` (`ShortKey`): `secret.jws_hmac_verify(key, alg, b"", tag)` with a zero tag of the algorithm's length must not answer `ShortKey`;
- `leeway_s > MAX_LEEWAY_S`;
- `max_token` above `MAX_TOKEN`;
- a `Static` document that `jwks_public` refuses or that has no usable key;
- a `Remote` URL that is not `https`;
- `every_ms` outside 60 s..24 h (default 300 s, GUESS), `stale_ms` outside `2 × every_ms`..7 d (GUESS), `fetch_ms` outside 1..30 s, or `max_life_s` outside 1..=86400 (ceiling and default 3600 both GUESS).

**Keys (v2.70).** An issuer is either asymmetric or symmetric, never both.
- **Asymmetric.** A `Static` or `Remote` issuer verifies `EdDsa`, `Es256` and `Rs256` against public keys only. A JWKS `oct` member fails the document, so a key server never delivers a MAC key.
- **Symmetric.** A `Mac` issuer verifies `Hs256`, `Hs384` and `Hs512` with sealed keys whose only use is `verify`. The keys reach the binding in its `G` (§15.1).

v2.60 refused HMAC because a place holding the MAC key could mint every token it accepts. That reason does not hold for a `verify`-use `Secret`. The runtime refuses it every operation that makes a tag (`Denied`), which `verifier` checks, and application code never reads its bytes (TLS-5). So the place's own code cannot mint.

What remains is HMAC's weaker guarantee, and accepting it is the operator's choice. The key's bytes exist at the issuer and at the place's grant source (a host file), so whoever reads either can mint tokens the place accepts. A public key leaves nothing to steal. This matches the treatment of HMAC elsewhere: a K-2 claim function verifies an HMAC webhook with a `verify`-use key in the same way (http.md K-2).

Algorithm confusion stays impossible by construction:
- a `Mac` issuer's keys include no public key, and its `allow` no asymmetric algorithm;
- an asymmetric issuer's set includes no MAC key, and its `allow` no HS algorithm;
- besides both, `std.jwt` checks the key's family.

`none` has no `Alg` (`std.jwt`).

**Mac keys and rotation (v2.70).** The keys are fixed for the place's life. A token's `kid` selects its key by `std.jwt.find`'s rule: a token without a `kid` selects the only key, and is `AmbiguousKey` when there are several. A `kid` that names no key is always `Rejected`, never `Unavailable`, since the set never changes. A key is rotated by redeploy:
1. deploy with {old, new};
2. the issuer switches to signing with the new key;
3. after the old tokens' `max_life_s + leeway_s`, deploy with {new}.

Rotation in place is J-D11.

**One issuer per binding.** A place has one `Wire`, so it trusts one issuer. Several issuers would need the binding to route by the unverified `iss`. That is additive: an `Issuer` list and a `std.jwt` peek at the payload's `iss`. It is left to a later slice (§15.9, OPEN).

**The key set.** `Seed` carries the binding's own `Clock` (plain and shareable, so the bind reads time without a grant), the set, and two monotonic instants (`host.mono_ns`), so a wall clock set back extends neither:
- `good_until_mono_ns`, from which the set is stale: the fetch's success reading plus `stale_ms`, a saturating sum;
- `confirmed_until_mono_ns`, until which the set is known current: the fetch's success reading plus `every_ms + every_ms/10 + fetch_ms`, a saturating sum.

Both are u64 max for a static set. `keys: None` means no set has been verified yet. The bind compares both itself, so the staleness bound holds even if the refresher is starved or has ended. `std.jwt` gains (slice J1) `PublicKeys: shareable + send`, built by `jwks_public(text)` from a JWK Set. It holds RSA, EC P-256 and Ed25519 public keys only; an `oct` member fails the document. `verify_jwt_public(token, r, keys)` behaves as `verify_jwt_in` does and uses `find`'s kid rule.

**Static source.** The document is parsed once by `verifier` and published once.

**Remote source and refresh.** `verifier` spawns one refresher task under `tasks`. It is the only holder of the `Net`. It is bounded as follows:
- **Rate.** It fetches at most once per `every_ms`, plus jitter drawn uniformly from 0..every_ms/10 so that places restarted together spread out. After a failure it retries at `min(every_ms, 5 s × 2^n)`. Nothing a client sends starts a fetch: a token whose `kid` is not in the set (`NoKey`) is refused (`Rejected`, or `Unavailable` once the set is unconfirmed, §15.3) and triggers nothing, so the fetch rate never depends on the bind rate or on attacker input.
- **Size and time.** Each fetch is one `GET` through `std.http_fetch` with deadline `fetch_ms`, redirects refused, and a body of at most `std.jwt.MAX_JWKS`. A larger body, a non-200 status, a body `jwks_public` refuses, or a set with no usable key is a failed fetch.
- **Rotation.** A successful fetch publishes the new set whole. A key removed at the issuer stops verifying at the next publish. A key added at the issuer verifies from the next publish. The issuer's rotation contract is the usual JWKS one: publish a new key at least `every_ms + every_ms/10` before signing with it. A token signed with a key the place has not fetched yet is `Rejected`, or `Unavailable` once the set is unconfirmed, and the SDK path for that is §15.6.
- **Staleness.** A failed fetch keeps the last good set and republishes nothing. From `good_until_mono_ns` on, every bind answers `Unavailable` until a fetch succeeds and publishes a new seed. A verifier never accepts a token on a set older than `stale_ms`, and never rejects a token only because the set is missing.
- **Before the first set.** `verifier` waits for one fetch for at most `fetch_ms`. If that succeeds, it returns with the set published. Otherwise it returns with `keys: None` and the refresher keeps trying. The place starts and answers `Unavailable` until then, so a key-server outage at deploy is a 1013/503 window, not a failed start.
- **Teardown.** The refresher is a child of the place's task group. It ends with the place, and it is never a reason for the place to stop.

### 15.3 Verification

`bind(g, credential)` reads `s = latest.read(g.seed)` once (v2.70), then does the following, in order, and its first refusal is its answer:

1. Read `wall_ms` and `mono_ns` once from `s.clock`; every later step uses these readings. If `s.keys` is `None` or `mono_ns >= s.good_until_mono_ns`, answer `Unavailable`. (v2.70) A `Mac` issuer's verifier publishes one seed with `keys: None` and both instants at u64 max, and this `keys` test is skipped when `g.mac` is non-empty.
2. Strip an `Authorization` scheme. If the credential starts with `Bearer` followed by one space, with the scheme ASCII case-insensitive (RFC 6750 §2.1), drop that prefix. The SDK sends the bare compact token in Hello and `Bearer <token>` over HTTP, so one token gives one Caller on both transports (L29). If the rest is longer than `max_token` or is not ASCII, answer `Rejected`.
3. Read the clock and verify:
   - `now_secs = wall_ms / 1000` (floor), from the step 1 reading.
   - Call `std.jwt.verify_jwt_public(token, Rules { allow, now_secs, leeway_secs: leeway_s, iss: Some(iss), aud: Some(aud), require_exp: true }, keys)`. (v2.70) For a `Mac` issuer, call `std.jwt.verify_jwt_in` with the same `Rules` over `std.jwt.key_set` of `with_kid(hmac_key(secret.share(k.key)), k.kid)` for each `k` in `g.mac`.
   - The signature is checked first, with the header's `alg` in `allow` and in the key's family, and `crit` refused. The claims are read only after the signature verifies (`std.jwt`).
   - An unknown `kid` (`NoKey`) is `Rejected` while `mono_ns < s.confirmed_until_mono_ns`, otherwise `Unavailable`. `AmbiguousKey` is always `Rejected`.
   - When `issuer.typ` is `Some(t)`, the header's `typ` must equal `t`, ASCII case-insensitive, with `application/` prefixed to a value that has no `/` (RFC 7515 §4.1.9); otherwise `Rejected`.
   - If `exp > now_secs + leeway_s + max_life_s` (saturating), answer `Rejected`.
4. Map the principal (§15.4).
5. Compute the expiry: `expires_at_ms = (exp + leeway_s) × 1000`, with checked arithmetic. An overflow answers `Rejected`.

**One instant for both checks.** `std.jwt` accepts while `exp > now_secs − leeway_s`, which is `now_secs < exp + leeway_s`. For integer `wall_ms` that is exactly `wall_ms < (exp + leeway_s) × 1000`, which is the runner's own test (`wall_ms >= expires_at_ms` refuses, CHANGES-v2.57). So bind refusal and session expiry fall at the same millisecond, and L64's vectors sit on it. Leeway extends a session by at most `leeway_s`. That is the stated skew bound: no more than `MAX_LEEWAY_S` = 300 s, default 60 s, the default SpacetimeDB uses (§15.10). `nbf` and `iat` use the same leeway the other way (`std.jwt`).

**Refusals.** Step 1 and an unconfirmed `kid` miss are the only `Unavailable`. Every other refusal is `Rejected`, which is 1008 or 401. The binding never reports which check failed (CHANGES-v2.24 decision log), so the table is for tests and diagnostics, not for the wire:

| Cause | `std.jwt.Error` or `jwtbind` check |
|---|---|
| no verified set, or a stale one | step 1, `Unavailable` |
| too long, not ASCII, or a bad scheme | `jwtbind` step 2 |
| bad structure | `Malformed(_)` (size, segments, encoding, UTF-8, JSON, not an object, duplicate member, missing `alg`) |
| `none`, or an algorithm outside `allow` (HS* at an asymmetric issuer, any asymmetric algorithm at a `Mac` issuer, v2.70) | `UnsupportedAlg`; `WrongKey` (the key's family or pinned alg differs) |
| a `crit` header | `UnsupportedCrit` |
| unknown `kid` | `NoKey`: `Rejected` while `mono_ns < confirmed_until_mono_ns`, otherwise `Unavailable`; always `Rejected` at a `Mac` issuer (v2.70) |
| ambiguous `kid` | `AmbiguousKey`, always `Rejected` |
| wrong `typ` | `jwtbind` step 3, when `issuer.typ` is set |
| `exp` more than `leeway_s + max_life_s` ahead | `jwtbind` step 3 |
| bad signature | `BadSignature` |
| expired | `Expired` |
| not yet valid | `NotYetValid` (`nbf` or `iat` past `now + leeway`) |
| wrong `iss`, wrong `aud`, no `exp` | `Mismatch(Iss)`, `Mismatch(Aud)`, `Mismatch(Exp)` |
| no `sub`, or a bad `sub` | §15.4 |
| expiry overflow | step 5 |
| any other `std.jwt.Error` | `Rejected` |

### 15.4 The principal

- **Issuer.** `Caller.issuer` is the configured `issuer` byte, never derived from the token. The verified `iss` equals the configured string, so the issuer number names exactly one `iss`. Issuer numbers are the place's fixed table (§3 "Caller"). A number is never reused for another `iss`. That is an operator rule that nothing enforces (J-D7).
- **Subject.** `Caller.subject` is the bytes of the `sub` claim, verbatim (UTF-8, as decoded). A token with no `sub`, an empty `sub`, or a `sub` over `SUBJECT_MAX` = 64 bytes is `Rejected`. The binding never truncates or hashes a `sub`, so two distinct `sub` values never share a Caller, and an entry can compare `c.subject` with an id an application stored. RFC 7519 §4.1.2 scopes `sub` to its issuer, which the issuer byte supplies.
- **Expiry.** `expires_at_ms` comes from `exp` (§15.3 step 5). There is no `None` expiry for a JWT Caller.
- **Nothing else enters the Caller.** Other claims (`jti`, scopes, roles) are not part of the principal. An entry that needs them reads them from its own tables, keyed by the Caller (D8 (c)). Passing claims to entries would make a second, per-session authority beside the Caller, and would put token bytes into call arguments that the store records.

### 15.5 Refresh at expiry

- **WS.** As CHANGES-v2.57 has it: at `expires_at_ms` the owner latches, and an established session closes with 4401 `AuthExpired`.
- **HTTP.** A one-shot request whose token has expired gets 401 at bind. An SSE stream ends with `event: expired` (L63, still OPEN).
- **Bound after rotation.** A key removed at the issuer admits no new bind after the next publish, or after `stale_ms` while fetches fail. A session bound under it ends within `max_life_s + 2 × leeway_s` of its bind. There is no earlier revocation.
- **No renewal in place.** A session never takes a new token. A fresh token is a new Hello, and the SDK replays pending work only when Welcome names the same Caller. A refreshed JWT from the same issuer with the same `sub` gives the same Caller (§15.4), so dedup and pending work survive token refresh.

### 15.6 The SDK delta (docs/spec/sdk.md)

docs/spec/sdk.md's credential recovery is unchanged for 1008, 4401 and 401. v2.60 adds one case. J2 implements the runner response and J5 implements client recovery:
- **New case: 1013 before Welcome, or HTTP 503 at bind.** This is not a credential failure. The client keeps its bytes, does not call the source and does not clear images. It reconnects with jittered backoff (the transport-failure path), and an SSE consumer re-requests the same way.
- **No change for rotation.** A token signed with a key the place has not fetched yet is a 1008 or 401 while the place's set is confirmed (a 1013 or 503 after, §15.3), and a source that returns the same bytes still ends in `Unbound`. The rotation contract is on the issuer (§15.2): it publishes a key at least `every_ms + every_ms/10` before signing with it, as OIDC providers do. A client-side wait cannot cover the window, which is the place's `every_ms` (up to 24 h) plus fetch backoff, and it would delay `Unbound` for every binding, not only JWT.

### 15.7 Tapes and replay

There are two records, and the binding's outcome goes on neither as a verdict to trust.

- **Store call records (§7).** Unchanged. A record holds the Caller in its stream key (`caller_key ‖ stream`) or claim key, never the token. Re-execution decodes the Caller from the key (L30) and never runs the binding, so it never verifies again, never reads a live key and never reads the clock for the binding. L31 holds for JWTs: no store file holds a byte of the token or of a fetched JWKS document. The key set is never written to the store; it lives only in the `Latest` and the refresher.
- **Recordings (docs/recording.md).** A recording build records the run's inputs at the replay point, and the binding's inputs are all such inputs:
  - the Hello credential or `Authorization` value, as a received delivery;
  - the bind child's `wall_ms` and `mono_ns` readings, and the refresher's `mono_ns` reading at each fetch success, as clock deliveries;
  - the refresher's JWKS response, as a `Net` delivery of received plaintext, public keys only.

  Replay delivers those recorded values. The binding therefore re-runs against the recorded document and the recorded clock and reaches the recorded answer, with no live key server and no live clock. A replay that took either one live would fail to compare rather than silently re-decide. The token bytes are on the recording, which is credential-bearing by class (docs/recording.md "Credentials"): it is never redacted in place, and `hirsel tape export` prints argument and payload bytes only with `--include-credentials`. No private key exists in this design. (v2.70) A MAC key is a sealed `Secret`, so a tape holds its designation and the recorded `SECRET_MAC` verdicts (docs/recording.md), never its bytes, and a replay reaches the recorded verdict with no key.
  - The bind runs in a spawned child task of the session. Recording it by unit stream is unit-tapes-spec-2339's U2–U4; until those slices land, the replay case of L62 and L66 stays OPEN, as CHANGES-v2.57 says.
- **Nothing else.** The runner logs no token, `kid`, claim or refusal cause. The diagnostic a place emits for a refused bind names the refusal row of §15.3 and the issuer byte only.

### 15.8 Decisions

| # | Decision | Chosen | /wholehog end-state | Reversible? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| J-D1 | Where JWT verification lives | a library binding (`jwtbind`) over `std.jwt`; the runner stays auth-free (rule 13) | the same: authentication is one ordinary binding, with no verifier in ingress | yes: a package boundary |
| J-D2 | How a binding gets a changing key set | (v2.70) a `latest.handle` in the `G` that the `bind` mint makes per bind; v2.60 to v2.69's `wire_bind` seed and `runner.wire`'s plain seed are deleted | the same as every other use: authority arrives as a `G` minted per use (R6), so binding, claims, calls and attempts take one route | yes: config and API only; reverting restores `wire_bind` and `B`. The cost (CHANGES-v2.70 F6) is GUESS until J9 |
| J-D3 | A binding that cannot decide | `BindRefusal = Rejected \| Unavailable`; 1013 and 503 | the same: an outage is never a credential failure | yes: one enum, with SDK handling |
| J-D4 | Algorithms, and bindings keyed by a `Secret` | **CLOSED (v2.70).** A binding gets a sealed key as a `secret.share` holder in its `bind` mint's `G`, and claim functions, granted calls and attempts get theirs through their own mints. `jwtbind` verifies `EdDSA`, `ES256` and `RS256` from a public set, or `HS256`, `HS384` and `HS512` from `verify`-only sealed keys (`Source.Mac`), never both at one issuer (§15.2 "Keys"). Checked: http.md K-2's examples and the bind route type-check and run (CHANGES-v2.70) | the same: one route (a mint per use) for every authority, and a verifier that holds no key able to mint | yes: config and API only |
| J-D5 | Refresh trigger | a timer only; a `kid` miss never fetches | the same: the fetch rate is independent of input | yes: a rate-limited `kid`-miss fetch is additive |
| J-D6 | Expiry instant | `(exp + leeway) × 1000`, with one instant for bind and session | the same | yes: one line, but L64's vectors change |
| J-D7 | Caller | configured issuer byte and verbatim `sub` (1–64 B). "A number is never reused for another `iss`" is an operator rule that nothing enforces: a redeploy that breaks it hands the old issuer's rows to the new one | the same | **no**: subjects are written into stream keys and application rows, and changing the mapping orphans every stored reply and row |
| J-D8 | Issuers per place | one | an `Issuer` list routed by verified `iss` | yes: additive |
| J-D9 | Claims beyond `sub` | not passed to entries | the same: authorization is entry code over the Caller (D8) | yes |
| J-D10 | Rotation | the issuer pre-publishes keys at least one refresh interval ahead; the SDK is unchanged | the same: the place's refresh interval is unknown to a client | yes: a place-supplied retry hint would be additive |
| J-D11 | Rotating a sealed key (v2.70) | by redeploy, with old and new keys held by `kid` for overlap (§15.2). **OPEN:** rotation in place | a host-pushed `Secret` holder that a mint shares, as Envoy SDS pushes a secret to a running listener, if a deployment needs rotation without a restart | yes: additive |

| Slice | Size | Depends on | Model | What |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| J1 | S | — | Sol | `std.jwt`: `PublicKeys: shareable + send`, `jwks_public` and `verify_jwt_public`, and `Allow` declared `shareable` so that `jwtbind.Issuer` and `Seed` can be; `verify_jwt_public` returns `Jwt`, whose `header.typ` J3 checks; `core/std/api/jwt.md` documents them |
| J2 | M | — | Sol | runner: `BindRefusal`, 1013 and 503, and `wire_bind` over `Latest[B]` with `B: shareable + send`; `runner.wire` is unchanged (a plain `G` copied into each bind); update the bind child, `copy_wire`, the harness gateway's two `wire_bind` call sites and the tests that bind; rename the checker refusal `wire_bind_nonplain` to `wire_bind_nonshareable` (L36). Every binding and every direct caller of one moves to `BindRefusal`: `runner.ingress.caller`, the harness gateway's direct call (gateway.hirsel) and `gateway_http.hirsel`, and the test bindings in `runner_calls`, the HTTP op law and the SSE law. `runner.bind_credential` takes a view of `B` (for a `runner.wire` front, its copy of `G`) and returns `Result[BoundCaller, BindRefusal]`, so a front maps `Unavailable` to its own 1013 or 503. K-2 claim functions are unchanged |
| J3 | M | J1, J2 | Sol | package `jwtbind`: `Issuer` (with `typ` and `max_life_s`), `Source`, `Seed` (with `good_until_mono_ns` and `confirmed_until_mono_ns`), `verifier`, the refresher and `bind` |
| J4 | S | J3 | Sol | measure `BIND_STEPS` and `BIND_BYTES` on an RSA-8192 token of `max_token` bytes and replace the GUESS values; no other benchmark |
| J5 | S | J2 | Sol | SDK: the 1013/503 case (§15.6) |
| J6 | — | U2–U4 (docs/recording.md) | — | the replay halves of L62 and L66 |
| J7 | M | v2.69's `Mint` build | Opus | runner (v2.70): `Grants.bind`, `Wire[G]` and `runner.wire(bind, ..)`. Delete `wire_bind`, `WireSeed` and `PlaceConfig`'s `B`. Add `BindNoGrant` and the bind mint refusals (CHANGES-v2.70 F5). `copy_wire` copies only the binding. Move every `runner.wire` and `wire_bind` call site to a `bind` mint (13 call lines under core/ outside runner-binding.hirsel, at fdeb99ef). Add L36's v2.70 cases. Delete `wire_bind_nonshareable` and `law_bind_only`'s `wire_bind` half. `runner.bind_credential` becomes `bind_credential[G: send](tasks, clock, take g: G, bind, take credential, bytes_budget, steps)`, which takes an owned `G` and drops it when the bind ends. `operate`'s bind path passes the `bind` mint's `G`. A front outside `operate` (the harness gateway, `gateway_http.hirsel`, `wire/admission.hirsel`) makes its own `G` for each bind, since it is application code with its own authority. `runner.ingress.caller` takes `()` as its `G` |

### 15.10 Laws

Every token in these laws is made by an outside tool and checked into the test's fixtures with its claims written out by hand. The tools are `openssl` (`genpkey`, `pkeyutl`) for the RSA, P-256 and Ed25519 keys and signatures, and Python's `cryptography` package, run once, to assemble the compact JWS. The expected answer of each case is derived from the RFC and the hand-written claims, never from `std.jwt`'s own output. `std.jwt`'s own laws (`core/std/tests/jwt.hirsel`) already cover `none`, algorithm confusion within `std.jwt`, malformed structure and the claim checks. These laws cover only what the binding adds, as cases in `runner_calls` (`core/programs/runner/runner_calls.hirsel`), the harness L36 and L62 run in, with a virtual `Clock`.

| Law | Statement | Mutants it catches | Oracle |
|---|---|---|---|
| L64 `jwt_expiry_instant` | With `exp` = E and leeway L, a bind at `wall_ms` = (E + L) × 1000 − 1 gives Welcome with `expires_at_ms` = (E + L) × 1000, and that session closes 4401 at wall (E + L) × 1000 exactly. A bind at (E + L) × 1000 is 1008. A token whose `(exp + L) × 1000` overflows u64 is 1008. With `max_life_s` = M, a token with `exp` = `now_secs` + L + M binds, and one with `exp` one second later is 1008 | `exp_ms_no_leeway` (`expires_at_ms = E × 1000`); `floor_mismatch` (rounds `wall_ms / 1000` up); `unchecked_mul`; `max_life_unchecked` | hand arithmetic on E = 1767225600 and L = 60 |
| L65 `jwt_caller_map` | An openssl-signed ES256 token with `sub` "user-42" binds `Caller(issuer: 9, subject: b"user-42")` over Hello and over HTTP `Bearer` (and `bearer`). The same `sub` in a second token with a later `exp` binds the same Caller. `sub` absent, empty or 65 bytes is 1008. A token from another `iss` or `aud` is 1008. With `typ: Some("at+jwt")`, the same claims under header `typ: "JWT"` are 1008 | `sub_truncated`; `issuer_from_token`; `bearer_case_sensitive`; `iss_unchecked`; `typ_unchecked` | the hand-written claims and headers |
| L66 `jwt_rotation` | Set A = {k1}. Inside the confirmed window, a k2-signed token is 1008 and starts no fetch (the fixture server's request count is unchanged). After the refresher's next fetch returns {k1, k2}, the same token binds. After a fetch returns {k2}, a k1 token is 1008 | `kid_miss_fetch` (a miss triggers a fetch); `merge_sets` (old keys kept after rotation) | the fixture server's request log and the hand-built JWKS documents |
| L67 `jwt_unavailable` | With the fixture key server down from start, a Hello is Close 1013 and an HTTP call is 503, never 1008 or 401. After a good fetch and then `stale_ms` of virtual time with the server down and the refresher task parked, the same holds. After the first good fetch, the token binds. After a good fetch, advancing virtual mono by `stale_ms` while setting wall back by `stale_ms` makes the bind 1013. With set {k1} and the server down past the confirmed window, a k2 token is 1013 and a k1 token with a bad signature is 1008 | `unavailable_as_rejected`; `stale_never` (a stale set keeps verifying); `stale_by_refresher` (only the refresher enforces staleness); `stale_on_wall`; `nokey_rejected_in_outage` | the close codes of §15.1 table and the fixture's request log |
| L68 `jwt_no_token_at_rest` | L31's byte search, extended: after calls bound by a JWT, no file in the store directory contains the token's signature segment, its payload segment, or the base64url `n` or `x` value of any key in the fixture JWKS | `claims_in_args` (J-D9 broken) | the byte search |
| L69 `jwt_config_refused` | `verifier` returns `ConfigError` for `issuer` 1, an HS256 entry in `allow` with a `Static` or `Remote` source, `leeway_s` 301, an `http:` URL, and a `Static` document holding an `oct` key. (v2.70) With a `Mac` source it refuses each of these: a `mac`-use key (`KeyUse`); a 31-byte `verify` key under HS256 and a 47-byte one under HS384 (`ShortKey`; RFC 7518 §3.2: a key at least the hash's size); `Es256` in `allow` (`Asymmetric`); an empty list, five keys, and a repeated `kid` (`MacKeys`). It accepts a 32-byte key under HS256 | `hmac_admitted` (HS* with a public source); `anon_issuer`; `mac_can_sign`; `short_key_admitted`; `mixed_issuer` | the list in §15.2 and RFC 7518 §3.2's minimum |
| L71 `jwt_hmac_bind` (v2.70) | A `Mac` issuer (number 9) with key k1 = `"hirsel-bind-check-key-32-bytes!!"` (`verify` use, `kid` "k1") and HS256. A token signed by Python's `hmac` over the hand-written claims `{"iss":"hirsel-test","aud":"place","sub":"user-42","exp":1767225660}` binds `Caller(9, b"user-42")` over Hello and over HTTP `Bearer`, at virtual wall 1767225600000. The same claims tagged with another 32-byte key are 1008. The same claims under an `RS256` header are 1008. With keys {k1, k2}, a k2 token with `kid` "k2" binds, a token with no `kid` is 1008 (`AmbiguousKey`), and one whose `kid` is "k3" is 1008 and never 1013. | `hmac_kid_ignored` (the binding verifies with the first key whatever the `kid`); `mac_nokey_unavailable` (an unknown `kid` at a `Mac` issuer is 1013); `mac_allow_unchecked` (a `Mac` issuer accepts an `RS256` header) | Python's `hmac` and `base64` over the hand-written claims; std.jwt's own RFC 7515 A.1 case (`core/std/tests/jwt.hirsel`) already covers JWS-level HS256 with a `verify` key, so it is not repeated |

**Status (v2.70):** slice J8 is built: `std.jwt.key_set`, `Source.Mac`, `MacKey`, `Grant`, `grant`, `share` and `bind(Grant, ..)`, with `seed` deleted. L69's v2.70 cases and L71 `jwt_hmac_bind` run green in `jwtbind_laws.hirsel` (Cranelift), and were checked once by hand against mutants: `hmac_kid_ignored`, `mac_nokey_unavailable`, a `check_family` that passes any `allow` at a `Mac` issuer, a short key admitted and a `mac`-use key admitted each fail a case. `mac_allow_unchecked` cannot be built in jwtbind: adding `RS256` to the bind's `allow` still gives 1008 because std.jwt refuses an `RS256` header against HMAC keys, so that case pins std.jwt's key-family check. L71's Hello case runs a place on the law's virtual clock, whose bind mint shares the `Grant`'s sealed key into each bind. Its HTTP case is the `Bearer ` credential the runner passes on, as in L65. **Status:** `test_j2_binding` covers L67's runner response codes with a fixture binding. L64-L67 and L69 run in jwtbind's own tests (`core/programs/jwtbind/jwtbind_laws.hirsel`, since `runner` cannot bind `jwtbind`). `hirsel test` runs them natively; J4 also ran them once on the evaluator, where all passed, but no evaluator gate runs them yet. L68 runs on L31's disk harness (`store_crash`'s `no_jwt_at_rest`, `core/tools/runner_rest.py`) over a fresh openssl key and token per run. It also searches the harness output and a marker claim, so `claims_in_args` is caught; a copy that prints the token fails it. The session owner's bind term holds the hello's bind child (4096 + `BIND_BYTES`, CHANGES-v2.71 B5), so the harness leaves `n_granted` alone. Recordings are out of scope, as they are for L31, because they carry credentials by class (§15.7). The replay half of L66 waits on J6. **Not repeated here:** the SDK's 1013 handling is slice J5's case in the existing SDK recovery suite, against a scripted place.

### 15.11 Prior art (each source opened)

- **Convex** (ref-convex-backend, crates/authentication/src/lib.rs). It picks a provider by the token's unverified `iss` and `aud` before verifying (:160-195), and fetches OIDC discovery or a custom JWKS on each validation, with a TODO for a cache (:207, :312). **ADAPTED:** issuer and subject as identity (as in §11). **NOT TAKEN:** per-validation fetch, which lets the fetch rate follow the bind rate, and routing on unverified claims (J-D8 keeps one issuer).
- **SpacetimeDB** (ref-spacetimedb, crates/core/src/auth/token_validation.rs). A caching OIDC validator refreshes every 300 s and expires entries after 7,200 s (:199-207). It accepts ES256 and RS256 by key family and refuses HMAC and Ed keys for OIDC (:135-141). It allows 60 s of leeway on `exp` (:164-167). When a `kid` is missing it tries keys without a kid in turn (:298-320). **ADOPTED:** a timer refresh with a bounded stale window, an asymmetric-only allow-list, and 60 s default leeway. **NOT TAKEN:** trying keys in turn (`std.jwt.find` never does), and refusing Ed25519 (`std.jwt` verifies it).
- **Goose** (ref-goose). It has no JWT, JWKS or binding code (a grep for "jwt" and "jwks" finds nothing). **NOT TAKEN:** nothing to take.
- Envoy's `jwt_authn` filter was not in the local clone, so it is not cited.
- (v2.70, J-D4) **systemd credentials** (systemd.exec(5), `LoadCredential=`). A unit's credentials are read-only files that are placed at unit start and visible only to that unit. A new value takes a restart. **ADOPTED:** a host file as the grant source, and rotation by restart (J-D11).
- **Cloudflare Workers secrets** (developers.cloudflare.com, "Secrets"). A secret is an `env` binding the worker reads, and `wrangler secret put` creates and deploys a new version. **ADAPTED:** the key reaches application code as a binding value. Hirsel's is a sealed `Secret` whose bytes the code never reads, not a string.
- **Envoy SDS** (envoyproxy.io, "Secret discovery service"). Secrets are pushed over gRPC or watched as files and rotated without a restart. A listener stays inactive until its secrets have arrived. **NOT TAKEN** for now: rotation in place is J-D11. The inactive-until-fetched rule matches `jwtbind`'s `Unavailable` before the first set.
- **Vault Agent injector** (developer.hashicorp.com, "Agent Sidecar Injector"). An init container and a sidecar render secrets to a shared in-memory volume, and the application re-reads the files. **NOT TAKEN:** that design leaves the bytes readable by the application.
- **Goose** (ref-goose): no secret-delivery or HMAC binding code (a grep for "secret", "hmac" and "credential" finds no request authenticator). **NOT TAKEN:** nothing to take.
